# HTML vs Markdown for Agent Output: Key Findings

## Core Argument

The research demonstrates that HTML outperforms markdown when agents generate content meant for human consumption. The fundamental claim: spatial information and interactive elements are flattened in markdown, making comprehension harder.

## Specific Claims (No Quantitative Metrics Provided)

The page offers **no numerical data** on comprehension rates, speed improvements, or other measurable metrics. Instead, it presents qualitative arguments:

- **Spatial superiority**: "Diffs and call-graphs are spatial information; markdown flattens them"
- **Interaction over description**: "Motion and interaction can't be described, only felt. A throwaway page with the real easing curve...tells you in five seconds what a paragraph of prose never could"
- **Format alignment**: HTML is "the natural format" for design systems since it's "the medium your design system ships in"

## Practical Recommendations

The page advocates 20 concrete HTML patterns replacing markdown use cases:

| Category | Examples |
|----------|----------|
| **Planning** | Side-by-side comparisons, timelines with diagrams |
| **Code Review** | Annotated diffs, module maps as boxes-and-arrows |
| **Design** | Interactive color swatches, component contact sheets |
| **Prototyping** | Animation sandboxes with tunable parameters |
| **Documentation** | Collapsible sections, tabbed code samples, margin glossaries |
| **Editing** | Drag-and-drop interfaces with export buttons |

## Key Pattern: User Stays in Loop

All custom editors end with "an export button that turns whatever you did in the UI back into something you can paste into the agent or commit"—embedding human feedback into iterative workflows.

## EXPLORATION REPORT: pde-skills Repository

### 1. Directory Layout

**Current state:**

```
skills/               — 10 skill directories
  ├── app-calibrate/
  ├── backlog-manage/

  ├── idea-triage/

hooks/                — 1 hook directory
  └── stop-the-line/

references/           — 7 reference documents
  ├── app-context-schema.md
  ├── confidence-meter.md
  ├── dora-metrics.md
  ├── ice-scoring.md
  ├── kano-classification.md
  ├── nfr-categories.md

docs/                 — Meta and guidance
  ├── authoring-learnings.md

**Existing skills (name + one-line description):**

1. **app-calibrate** — Creates or updates `docs/app-context.md` with app-specific baseline data (metrics, performance signals, user volume, problem areas)
2. **backlog-manage** — Lifecycle skill for the idea bank; review, curation, validation-slot promotion, KTLO tracking
3. **design-doc** — Produce a structured design document before non-trivial implementation
4. **idea-triage** — Intake gate for new ideas; interrogate, score (ICE), and route to idea bank or validation slot
5. **plan-review** — Review a plan, spec, design, or roadmap before approval; catch scope drift, unaddressed assumptions, missing operability
6. **planning-and-task-breakdown** — Decompose a design or spec into small, verifiable tasks with acceptance criteria and dependency ordering
7. **prototype-to-validate** — Build a throwaway artefact to answer one specific product question before committing engineering time
8. **roadmap-shape** — Build or review a roadmap using portfolio-theme classification and explicit capacity allocation
9. **using-this-pack** — Meta-skill for navigating pde-skills; identify which skill applies and explain core operating behaviours

---

### 2. Skill Anatomy Contract — `docs/skill-anatomy.md`

**Required frontmatter fields (exact schema):**

```yaml
---
name: <skill-name>
description: <1-2 sentences, includes trigger phrases>
pack: <product | engineering | meta>
lifecycle_stage: <define | plan | build | ship | verify | review | meta | reference>
principles_implemented:
  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
    id: <principle or rule id>
    bucket: <embedded | standalone | hook | sub-agent>
length_target: <e.g. 200-280>
author: Anton Babushkin
predecessor:
  repo: <URL or "none">
  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
---
```

**Required body sections (in exact order):**

1. Title — `# <Skill Name>`
2. Purpose — one paragraph
3. When to use — explicit trigger phrases and intents, with size-the-work rubric where relevant
4. When not to use — explicit non-triggers, with right alternative skill named
5. Inputs — what the skill expects to be given
6. Outputs — what it produces, including artefact path under `docs/`
7. Workflow — numbered steps with hard gates marked `[GATE]`
8. Artefact template — the markdown the skill produces (or variant: "Task list template", "Triage record template", etc.)
9. Common rationalisations — table of typical excuses and rebuttals
10. Red flags — bullet list of signs the skill is being violated
11. Verification / exit criteria — how to know the skill ran correctly
12. References — pointers to `references/` and to source principles

**Length rules:**
- Target: 100–300 lines
- Below 100: editor checks for under-specification (acceptable if genuine)
- Above 300: editor checks for redundancy, scope creep, or over-explanation
- **Hard cap: 350 lines**; beyond that, split

**Artefact template section:**
- Named section (can use descriptive variant like "Task list template" instead of "Artefact template")
- Must appear at required position (section 8)
- Examples: design-doc uses `Artefact template`, planning-and-task-breakdown uses `Task list template`, idea-triage uses `Artefact template`

**Meta-pack exemptions:**
- Skills with `pack: meta` are exempt from: `principles_implemented`, `predecessor` frontmatter, and body sections Inputs, Outputs, Artefact template, Common rationalisations, Red flags, Verification/exit criteria
- Must have: Title, Purpose, When to use, When not to use, References/lookup table

---

### 3. Voice and Artefact Structure — Two Representative Skills

#### **design-doc** (`/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/design-doc/SKILL.md`)

**Artefact template structure:** Uses standard YAML frontmatter + markdown sections. Template includes:
```markdown
# <title>
## Problem
## Context
## Constraints
## Alternatives considered
## Recommended approach
## Consequences
## Operability plan
## Open questions
```

**Voice characteristics:**
- **Directive and gate-oriented.** "A skeletal or absent Operability section means the doc is not ready for review. This gate does not pass until every sub-field has content."
- **Problem-first framing.** "It is the operational form of universal Principle 2 (design starts with the problem, not the stack): the seven-section structure mandated by Rule A2 makes solution-first approaches structurally incomplete."
- **High specificity on failure modes.** "A problem statement that slides into solution language ('users can't do X, so we should build Y') is sent back for rewrite."
- **Emphasis on named constraints.** "For each NFR, name its fitness function — the automated check that will verify it continuously (Rule A5). 'Fast,' 'reliable,' and 'scalable' are not NFRs."
- **Rationale-driven.** Explains *why* things matter, not just *that* they do.

**Quote samples:**
> "An agent or engineer cannot finish the artefact without working through Problem, Context, Constraints, Alternatives, and Operability in that order."

> "The Operability section is fully populated. The document is ready for review before implementation begins."

> "A design doc with only the chosen solution is incomplete by Rule A2."

#### **planning-and-task-breakdown** (`/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/planning-and-task-breakdown/SKILL.md`)

**Artefact template structure:** Simpler, more format-light. Template:
```markdown
# Tasks: <feature-slug>
Design doc: docs/designs/<feature-slug>.md
Last updated: <date>

## Task list
### Task 1 — Walking skeleton
**Description:** [one sentence]
**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
**Dependencies:** none

### Task N — [name]
...

## Open questions
```

**Voice characteristics:**
- **Verification-centric.** "Size is not about effort estimation; it is about verification granularity. Small tasks surface problems early."
- **One-sentence rule enforced with examples.** Example too big vs. split correctly provided in the workflow step itself.
- **Hard rules with explicit error handling.** "Any task that: cannot be described in one sentence / has more than one acceptance criterion / would take more than one working session (~4–6 hours) — is too big. Split it."
- **Dependencies treated as a pre-implementation safety check.** "Dependency discovery during a build is expensive; discovery before the build is cheap."
- **Acceptance criteria as constraint, not description.** "Criteria written before implementation are a spec — they constrain what you build. Criteria written after implementation describe what you built. The goal is the former."

**Quote samples:**
> "Each task has one observable outcome, one test, and one commit boundary. That is a correctly sized task."

> "A listed dependency that turns out not to exist costs nothing. An unlisted dependency that surfaces mid-sprint costs days."

> "Task size is not a trust issue — it is a verification issue. Big tasks fail silently. Small tasks fail loudly and early."

**Common voice patterns across both:**
- Active imperative: "Name the trigger," "List all candidates," "Write it out"
- Specificity over abstraction: numbered units, concrete examples, named constraints
- Gate language: `[GATE]` marks hard decision points; sections are present or the skill fails
- Rationale before rules: explains *why* the structure matters before declaring it mandatory
- No hedging: "must," "hard reject," "does not pass," not "should" or "ideally"

### 4. HTML Rendering & Presentation Scaffolding — Greenfield Status

**Result: GREENFIELD — no existing scaffolding**

No HTML, CSS, templates, or rendering logic anywhere in the repo.

Searches performed:
- `grep -r "html|render|artefact"` — found only: use of word "artefact" in documentation and governance docs (no rendering code)
- `find . -name "*.html"` — 0 results
- `find . -name "*.css"` — 0 results
- `find . -name "*.js"`, `*.ts`, `*.tsx"` — 0 results
- No template engines (hbs, ejs, etc.)

**What the repo produces:**
- Plain markdown files (`.md`) filed under `docs/` per skill spec
- Examples: `docs/design-docs/<slug>/design-doc.md`, `docs/idea-bank/<slug>.md`, `docs/tasks/<feature>.md`, `docs/roadmap.md`
- No pipeline to render or transform these to HTML
- All artefacts are markdown-native, reviewed as text

### 5. Skill Registration & Slash Command Surfacing

**Plugin registration** (`/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/.claude-plugin/plugin.json`):
```json
{
  "name": "pde",
  "description": "Workflow skills for small product-design-engineering teams.",
  "version": "0.1.0",
  "author": { "name": "Anton Babushkin" },
  "homepage": "https://github.com/ababushkin/pde-skills",
  "repository": "https://github.com/ababushkin/pde-skills",
  "license": "MIT",
  "commands": "./.claude/commands"
}
```

The `"commands"` field points to `./.claude/commands/`.

**Command registration** (example: `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/.claude/commands/design-doc.md`):
```markdown
---
description: "Run design-doc — structures significant engineering work before building: problem statement, approach options, chosen design, NFR constraints, operability plan."
argument-hint: "[work description]"
disable-model-invocation: true
---

@../../skills/design-doc/SKILL.md

Run the design-doc skill for the following work: $ARGUMENTS
```

Each skill has a command file with:
- **Frontmatter:** `description`, `argument-hint`, `disable-model-invocation: true`
- **Body:** `@../../skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md` reference + one-sentence invocation instruction using `$ARGUMENTS`

**Patterns observed:**
- One command file per skill (located in `.claude/commands/`)
- Each command file references the corresponding `SKILL.md` file
- `disable-model-invocation: true` means the command loads the skill directly, not as a language-model-generated response
- Argument hints help users understand what to pass: `[work description]`, `[design doc or spec]`, etc.

---

### Summary for Your Skill Design

You're building in a **pure markdown environment** with a clear skill anatomy contract. The repo expects:
- Markdown input (design docs, plans) → **your skill** → rich HTML output (interactive diffs, timelines, collapsible sections)
- No existing HTML rendering pipeline to integrate with
- Strong precedent for artefact-focused skills (design-doc, planning, idea-triage all produce filed markdown)
- Voice is direct, gate-driven, verification-centric, and rationale-first

**Files to reference when authoring:**
- `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/docs/skill-anatomy.md` — your authoring contract
- `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — reference for problem-first workflow + artefact template structure
- `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/planning-and-task-breakdown/SKILL.md` — reference for small-task verification + acceptance criteria pattern
- `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/idea-triage/SKILL.md` — reference for structured gate-based workflow + ICE scoring template

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/.claude/plans/expressive-knitting-quiche.md",
  "content": "# Plan: `render-html` skill — markdown artefacts → self-contained HTML\n\n## Context\n\nDesign docs, plans, ADRs, and roadmaps produced by the pde-skills pack are markdown by default. Markdown is fine for authoring and version control but degrades the review experience: alternatives that should sit side-by-side are stacked, task lists with dependencies flatten into bullet trees, and long docs lack navigation. Thariq Shihipar's \"HTML effectiveness\" piece (thariqs.github.io/html-effectiveness) argues the case directly — spatial content (diffs, comparisons, dependency maps, timelines) is flattened by markdown, and HTML is the right substrate when an agent's output is meant for human review.\n\nThe pack is currently 100% markdown (no HTML/CSS/JS anywhere — confirmed greenfield). Adding HTML rendering as a new skill (rather than retrofitting `design-doc`, `planning-and-task-breakdown`, etc.) keeps each skill single-purpose and lets one renderer cover every artefact type.\n\nUser-confirmed decisions:\n- **Output shape:** single self-contained `.html` file (inline CSS + JS, no network, no build step).\n- **Scope:** any structured markdown — skill infers structure rather than relying on a fixed schema.\n- **Interactivity:** rich-static + light JS — TOC nav, collapsibles, tabs, side-by-side comparison columns, annotated diffs, timeline diagrams. No editors/sandboxes/export buttons.\n\n## Recommended approach\n\nA new skill `render-html` that an agent invokes with an input markdown path; it emits `<source>.html` alongside the source `.md`. The skill is responsible for (a) recognising structural patterns in the markdown and (b) mapping each pattern to a corresponding HTML treatment from a catalogue. The CSS/JS skeleton and the pattern catalogue live in reference files so SKILL.md stays inside the 100–300 line budget.\n\n### Files to create\n\n1. **`skills/render-html/SKILL.md`** — the skill itself. Follows `docs/skill-anatomy.md` exactly.\n   - Frontmatter:\n     - `name: render-html`\n     - `description:` includes trigger phrases: \"render this doc as HTML\", \"make this reviewable\", \"convert design doc to HTML\", \"I want to share this plan\"\n     - `pack: engineering`\n     - `lifecycle_stage: review`\n     - `principles_implemented:`\n       - `eng-agentic` Principle 7 (memory lives in artefacts) — `bucket: standalone`\n       - `eng-universal` Principle 1 (shipped is not done; observed is done) — `bucket: standalone` — better review UX raises the rate at which outcomes are actually observed\n     - `length_target: 180-240`\n     - `predecessor: { repo: \"none\", skill: \"none\", relation: \"new\" }`\n   - Body sections (required order from `docs/skill-anatomy.md`):\n     - Purpose\n     - When to use — trigger phrases above, plus \"before a doc goes to review or sign-off\"\n     - When not to use — drafting (write md first), single-screen docs (overhead not worth it), anything the reviewer will edit (HTML round-trip not supported)\n     - Inputs — path to a markdown file with H1/H2 structure\n     - Outputs — `<source>.html` written alongside `<source>.md`; one file, self-contained, ≤ ~300KB\n     - Workflow with `[GATE]`s:\n       1. Read source md; confirm it has at least one H1 and structured sections — `[GATE]`\n       2. Inventory the structural patterns present (table of alternatives, numbered task list with dependencies, problem/context/decision/consequences blocks, ADR-style decision matrix, diff blocks, etc.)\n       3. For each pattern, select the matching HTML treatment from `references/html-patterns.md`\n       4. Compose one HTML file using the skeleton from `references/html-skeleton.md`\n       5. Verify: every md heading appears in HTML TOC; every md code block is present; file opens offline with no console errors — `[GATE]`\n       6. Write to `<source>.html`\n     - Artefact template — a minimal HTML stub showing the document shell (TOC sidebar, main column, footer with \"rendered from <source>.md at <date>\")\n     - Common rationalisations — table covering: \"the md is good enough\" / \"I'll use a static-site generator instead\" / \"I'll add export buttons later\" / \"skip the TOC for short docs\" — each with rebuttal\n     - Red flags — external CSS/JS links, file > 500KB, missing TOC, sections silently dropped, JS that breaks when opened via `file://`\n     - Verification / exit criteria — open in browser via `file://`, confirm no network requests, no console errors, every md heading reachable\n     - References — link to `references/html-patterns.md`, `references/html-skeleton.md`, and the thariq piece\n\n2. **`references/html-patterns.md`** — pattern catalogue. For each recognisable md pattern, the matching HTML treatment with a small code sample.\n   - Patterns to cover (derived from pde artefact shapes + the thariq piece):\n     - **Alternatives considered** (design-doc) → side-by-side comparison columns with shared row labels (Pros / Cons / Cost / Reversibility)\n     - **Task list with dependencies** (planning-and-task-breakdown) → vertical timeline with dependency arrows; collapsibles per task\n     - **Problem / Context / Constraints / Decision / Consequences** (design-doc, ADR) → coloured callout blocks with a sticky section nav\n     - **NFRs table** → sortable table with numeric columns highlighted\n     - **Open questions** → checkbox list with \"owner\" and \"due\" columns when present\n     - **Diff or before/after blocks** → two-column annotated diff\n     - **Roadmap (Now/Next/Later)** → three-column kanban-style layout\n     - **Idea-bank record (ICE scores)** → score chips with colour ramps\n   - Each entry: when to apply, the source md shape, the HTML/CSS snippet, accessibility notes (semantic tags, contrast).\n\n3. **`references/html-skeleton.md`** — the reusable shell.\n   - Single `<!doctype html>` template with:\n     - `<head>` containing inline `<style>` covering: system font stack, prose width cap (~70ch), responsive collapsibles via `<details>`, tab component (radio-input pattern, no JS needed), side-by-side columns via CSS grid, sticky TOC sidebar, print stylesheet, prefers-color-scheme dark mode.\n     - `<body>` skeleton: sticky header (title + last-rendered date), left TOC sidebar (auto-generated from H2/H3), main content column, footer with provenance line.\n     - Minimal `<script>` (≤ 50 lines): TOC scrollspy, copy-link-to-section buttons. No frameworks. ## Approved Plan (edited by user):
# Plan: `render-html` skill — markdown artefacts → self-contained HTML

## Context

Design docs, plans, ADRs, and roadmaps produced by the pde-skills pack are markdown by default. Markdown is fine for authoring and version control but degrades the review experience: alternatives that should sit side-by-side are stacked, task lists with dependencies flatten into bullet trees, and long docs lack navigation. Thariq Shihipar's "HTML effectiveness" piece (thariqs.github.io/html-effectiveness) argues the case directly — spatial content (diffs, comparisons, dependency maps, timelines) is flattened by markdown, and HTML is the right substrate when an agent's output is meant for human review.

The pack is currently 100% markdown (no HTML/CSS/JS anywhere — confirmed greenfield). Adding HTML rendering as a new skill (rather than retrofitting `design-doc`, `planning-and-task-breakdown`, etc.) keeps each skill single-purpose and lets one renderer cover every artefact type.

User-confirmed decisions:
- **Output shape:** single self-contained `.html` file (inline CSS + JS, no network, no build step).
- **Scope:** any structured markdown — skill infers structure rather than relying on a fixed schema.
- **Interactivity:** rich-static + light JS — TOC nav, collapsibles, tabs, side-by-side comparison columns, annotated diffs, timeline diagrams. No editors/sandboxes/export buttons.

## Recommended approach

A new skill `render-html` that an agent invokes with an input markdown path; it emits `<source>.html` alongside the source `.md`. The skill is responsible for (a) recognising structural patterns in the markdown and (b) mapping each pattern to a corresponding HTML treatment from a catalogue. The CSS/JS skeleton and the pattern catalogue live in reference files so SKILL.md stays inside the 100–300 line budget.

### Files to create

1. **`skills/render-html/SKILL.md`** — the skill itself. Follows `docs/skill-anatomy.md` exactly.
   - Frontmatter:
     - `name: render-html`
     - `description:` includes trigger phrases: "render this doc as HTML", "make this reviewable", "convert design doc to HTML", "I want to share this plan"
     - `pack: engineering`
     - `lifecycle_stage: review`
     - `principles_implemented:`
       - `eng-agentic` Principle 7 (memory lives in artefacts) — `bucket: standalone`
       - `eng-universal` Principle 1 (shipped is not done; observed is done) — `bucket: standalone` — better review UX raises the rate at which outcomes are actually observed
     - `length_target: 180-240`
     - `predecessor: { repo: "none", skill: "none", relation: "new" }`
   - Body sections (required order from `docs/skill-anatomy.md`):
     - Purpose
     - When to use — trigger phrases above, plus "before a doc goes to review or sign-off"
     - When not to use — drafting (write md first), single-screen docs (overhead not worth it), anything the reviewer will edit (HTML round-trip not supported)
     - Inputs — path to a markdown file with H1/H2 structure
     - Outputs — `<source>.html` written alongside `<source>.md`; one file, self-contained, ≤ ~300KB
     - Workflow with `[GATE]`s:
       1. Read source md; confirm it has at least one H1 and structured sections — `[GATE]`
       2. Inventory the structural patterns present (table of alternatives, numbered task list with dependencies, problem/context/decision/consequences blocks, ADR-style decision matrix, diff blocks, etc.)
       3. For each pattern, select the matching HTML treatment from `references/html-patterns.md`
       4. Compose one HTML file using the skeleton from `references/html-skeleton.md`
       5. Verify: every md heading appears in HTML TOC; every md code block is present; file opens offline with no console errors — `[GATE]`
       6. Write to `<source>.html`
     - Artefact template — a minimal HTML stub showing the document shell (TOC sidebar, main column, footer with "rendered from <source>.md at <date>")
     - Common rationalisations — table covering: "the md is good enough" / "I'll use a static-site generator instead" / "I'll add export buttons later" / "skip the TOC for short docs" — each with rebuttal
     - Red flags — external CSS/JS links, file > 500KB, missing TOC, sections silently dropped, JS that breaks when opened via `file://`
     - Verification / exit criteria — open in browser via `file://`, confirm no network requests, no console errors, every md heading reachable
     - References — link to `references/html-patterns.md`, `references/html-skeleton.md`, and the thariq piece

2. **`references/html-patterns.md`** — pattern catalogue. For each recognisable md pattern, the matching HTML treatment with a small code sample.
   - Patterns to cover (derived from pde artefact shapes + the thariq piece):
     - **Alternatives considered** (design-doc) → side-by-side comparison columns with shared row labels (Pros / Cons / Cost / Reversibility)
     - **Task list with dependencies** (planning-and-task-breakdown) → vertical timeline with dependency arrows; collapsibles per task
     - **Problem / Context / Constraints / Decision / Consequences** (design-doc, ADR) → coloured callout blocks with a sticky section nav
     - **NFRs table** → sortable table with numeric columns highlighted
     - **Open questions** → checkbox list with "owner" and "due" columns when present
     - **Diff or before/after blocks** → two-column annotated diff
     - **Roadmap (Now/Next/Later)** → three-column kanban-style layout
     - **Idea-bank record (ICE scores)** → score chips with colour ramps
   - Each entry: when to apply, the source md shape, the HTML/CSS snippet, accessibility notes (semantic tags, contrast).

3. **`references/html-skeleton.md`** — the reusable shell.
   - Single `<!doctype html>` template with:
     - `<head>` containing inline `<style>` covering: system font stack, prose width cap (~70ch), responsive collapsibles via `<details>`, tab component (radio-input pattern, no JS needed), side-by-side columns via CSS grid, sticky TOC sidebar, print stylesheet, prefers-color-scheme dark mode.
     - `<body>` skeleton: sticky header (title + last-rendered date), left TOC sidebar (auto-generated from H2/H3), main content column, footer with provenance line.
     - Minimal `<script>` (≤ 50 lines): TOC scrollspy, copy-link-to-section buttons. No frameworks. No CDN.
   - Hard rule documented: no `<link>` to external stylesheets, no `<script src=>` to external JS, no remote images (inline SVG or base64 only).

4. **`.claude/commands/render-html.md`** — slash-command wrapper, mirrors the existing pattern (e.g. `.claude/commands/design-doc.md`):
   ```
   ---
   description: "Render a markdown design doc or plan as a self-contained HTML file for review."
   argument-hint: "[path to markdown file]"
   disable-model-invocation: true
   ---

   @../../skills/render-html/SKILL.md

   Render the following markdown artefact as HTML: $ARGUMENTS
   ```

### Files to modify

- **`README.md`** — add `render-html` to the engineering/review skill table (one-line description + when to use).
- **`skills/using-this-pack/SKILL.md`** — add a routing entry: if user says "render", "make reviewable", "share this doc", "convert to HTML" → `render-html`.
- **`.claude-plugin/plugin.json`** — no change needed; commands directory is auto-scanned.

### Reuse / existing patterns to follow

- Skill anatomy contract: `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/docs/skill-anatomy.md` — frontmatter schema, section order, length rules, `[GATE]` notation.
- Voice / artefact-template style: `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` and `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/planning-and-task-breakdown/SKILL.md` — directive, gate-driven, rationale-first.
- Command-file shape: `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/.claude/commands/design-doc.md`.
- Reference-file style: `/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/references/portfolio-themes.md` and `kano-classification.md` — flat md, examples-heavy, no frontmatter.

### Out of scope (deliberately)

- Round-trip editing (HTML → md). The thariq piece's "export button" pattern is interesting but doubles skill complexity; defer until there's evidence of demand.
- Live preview / watch mode. Markdown-only repo, no toolchain.
- Diagram rendering from natural-language descriptions (e.g., mermaid → SVG). Use inline `<svg>` written by the agent when needed.
- Multi-doc site generation. Single artefact in, single file out.

## Verification

End-to-end test, done manually after implementation:

1. Pick an existing design doc that already lives in `docs/` (or create a small synthetic one with all the recognisable patterns: alternatives table, task list with deps, NFRs, open questions).
2. Invoke `/render-html <path>`.
3. Confirm:
   - Output file is written to `<source>.html`.
   - File opens via `file://` in Chrome and Safari with no network requests (DevTools Network tab empty).
   - No console errors or warnings.
   - TOC sidebar lists every H2; clicking jumps to the section.
   - Alternatives section renders side-by-side (not stacked).
   - Task list renders as a timeline with dependency edges.
   - Collapsibles expand/collapse without JS errors.
   - File size < 500KB.
   - Print preview renders cleanly (TOC hidden, page breaks reasonable).
4. Run the skill against a doc with an unrecognised pattern (e.g., a raw bulleted brainstorm) — confirm it degrades gracefully to a clean prose render with TOC, rather than failing or fabricating structure.
5. Re-render the same source — confirm output is deterministic enough that a diff shows only timestamp/provenance lines changing.

Skill-anatomy compliance check: SKILL.md is between 100 and 300 lines, all required sections present in required order, `[GATE]`s preserved, frontmatter validates.

Due: before starting Task 3.*\n- **Q3:** Output file naming when the source is `foo.md` and `foo.html` already exists: overwrite silently, prompt, or append a timestamp? *Owner: Anton. ---
name: skill-creator
description: Create new skills, modify and improve existing skills, and measure skill performance. Use when users want to create a skill from scratch, edit, or optimize an existing skill, run evals to test a skill, benchmark skill performance with variance analysis, or optimize a skill's description for better triggering accuracy.
---

# Skill Creator

A skill for creating new skills and iteratively improving them.

At a high level, the process of creating a skill goes like this:

- Decide what you want the skill to do and roughly how it should do it
- Write a draft of the skill
- Create a few test prompts and run claude-with-access-to-the-skill on them
- Help the user evaluate the results both qualitatively and quantitatively
  - While the runs happen in the background, draft some quantitative evals if there aren't any (if there are some, you can either use as is or modify if you feel something needs to change about them). Then explain them to the user (or if they already existed, explain the ones that already exist)
  - Use the `eval-viewer/generate_review.py` script to show the user the results for them to look at, and also let them look at the quantitative metrics
- Rewrite the skill based on feedback from the user's evaluation of the results (and also if there are any glaring flaws that become apparent from the quantitative benchmarks)
- Repeat until you're satisfied
- Expand the test set and try again at larger scale

Your job when using this skill is to figure out where the user is in this process and then jump in and help them progress through these stages. So for instance, maybe they're like "I want to make a skill for X". You can help narrow down what they mean, write a draft, write the test cases, figure out how they want to evaluate, run all the prompts, and repeat.

On the other hand, maybe they already have a draft of the skill. In this case you can go straight to the eval/iterate part of the loop.

Of course, you should always be flexible and if the user is like "I don't need to run a bunch of evaluations, just vibe with me", you can do that instead.

Then after the skill is done (but again, the order is flexible), you can also run the skill description improver, which we have a whole separate script for, to optimize the triggering of the skill.

Cool? Cool.

## Communicating with the user

The skill creator is liable to be used by people across a wide range of familiarity with coding jargon. If you haven't heard (and how could you, it's only very recently that it started), there's a trend now where the power of Claude is inspiring plumbers to open up their terminals, parents and grandparents to google "how to install npm". On the other hand, the bulk of users are probably fairly computer-literate.

So please pay attention to context cues to understand how to phrase your communication! In the default case, just to give you some idea:

- "evaluation" and "benchmark" are borderline, but OK
- for "JSON" and "assertion" you want to see serious cues from the user that they know what those things are before using them without explaining them

{
    {
    }
}
{
}
{
}
{
}

Proposal: `skills/render-html/evals/` (co-located with the skill, ignored by anatomy line-count checks). Confirm before Task 3. *Owner: Anton. Due: before Task 3.*\n- **Q2:** Pattern detection — does the skill use heading-name heuristics (e.g., \"Alternatives considered\" triggers the alternatives pattern), explicit fenced annotations (` ```pattern:alternatives `), or both? Heading-name heuristics are simpler but fragile to renames. Affects fixture design in Task 3. *Owner: Anton. Due: before Task 3.*\n- **Q3:** Output file naming when the source is `foo.md` and `foo.html` already exists: overwrite silently, prompt, or append a timestamp? Affects walking skeleton acceptance criterion. *Owner: Anton. Due: before Task 1.*\n- **Q4:** skill-creator's evals appear to want a runnable harness. Are we running evals in this conversation (i.e. live), or is it acceptable to define the fixtures + expectations and stop short of automated scoring for v0.1? *Owner: Anton. Due: before Task 3.*",
  "new_string": "## Resolved decisions\n\n- **Fixtures location:** `skills/render-html/evals/` (co-located with the skill). Safe because the Claude Code plugin loader only auto-loads `SKILL.md` from each skill directory — subdirectory contents are read on demand by the agent, not eagerly loaded into context. Confirmed against the loader behaviour observed in `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` discovery. Re-check if skill-creator publishes contrary best-practice guidance.\n- **Pattern detection:** hybrid — heading-name heuristics are the primary signal, with an optional explicit override via a fenced annotation (` ```pattern:alternatives `) for cases where authors want precision or where their headings don't match the catalogue. Rationale: pde skills already produce consistent heading names (design-doc enforces \"Problem\", \"Alternatives considered\", etc. by anatomy), so heading-name detection works out of the box for the most common inputs. The annotation escape hatch preserves the \"any structured md\" property without forcing every author to mark up their docs. Pure-heuristic loses on renames; pure-annotation breaks zero-effort rendering; hybrid avoids both failure modes.\n- **Output file collision:** overwrite with a confirmation prompt. When `<source>.html` exists, the skill must prompt before writing. Affects Task 1's acceptance criterion (below).\n- **Eval harness scope for v0.1:** define fixtures + expected-output descriptions and *eyeball* results during iteration. Defer automated scoring to v0.2. Context: skill-creator's full loop wants (a) fixture inputs, (b) machine-checkable expectations, (c) running claude-with-the-skill against each fixture, (d) automated pass/fail scoring, (e) iterate until scores stabilise. Automated scoring catches regressions when the pattern catalogue grows, but for the initial 8 patterns the iteration cost of eyeballing is lower than the cost of building the scoring harness. Revisit once we add a 9th pattern or a regression appears."
}

```
1	# Skill anatomy
2	
3	The authoring spec for `SKILL.md` files in `skills/<name>/`.
4	
5	## Frontmatter
6	
7	```yaml
8	---
9	name: <skill-name>
10	description: <1-2 sentences, includes trigger phrases>
11	pack: <product | engineering | meta>
12	lifecycle_stage: <define | plan | build | ship | verify | review | meta | reference>
13	principles_implemented:
14	  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
15	    id: <principle or rule id>
16	    bucket: <embedded | standalone | hook | sub-agent>
17	length_target: <e.g. 200-280>
18	author: Anton Babushkin
19	predecessor:
20	  repo: <URL or "none">
21	  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
22	  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
23	kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
24	changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
25	---
26	```
27	
28	## Sections
29	
30	In this exact order:
31	
32	1. **Title** — `# <Skill Name>`
33	2. **Purpose** — one paragraph
34	3. **When to use** — explicit trigger phrases and intents, with the size-the-work rubric where relevant
35	4. **When not to use** — explicit non-triggers, with the right alternative skill named
36	5. **Inputs** — what the skill expects to be given
37	6. **Outputs** — what it produces, including the artefact path under `docs/`
38	7. **Workflow** — numbered steps with hard gates marked `[GATE]`
39	8. **Artefact template** — the markdown the skill produces
40	9. **Common rationalisations** — table of typical excuses and rebuttals
41	10. **Red flags** — bullet list of signs the skill is being violated
42	11. **Verification / exit criteria** — how to know the skill ran correctly
43	12. **References** — pointers to `references/` and to source principles
44	
45	## Length
46	
47	Target 100–300 lines. Below 100, editor checks for under-specification (acceptable if genuinely complete). Above 300, editor checks for redundancy with other skills, scope creep, or over-explanation. Hard cap 350 lines; beyond that, split.
48	
49	## Artefact template section name
50	
51	The anatomy calls this section "Artefact template." Skills that produce a single named artefact may use a descriptive variant (e.g. "Task list template", "Finding template") — the name should identify the artefact type. The section must still appear in the required position.
52	
53	## Root rules files
54	
55	`PRODUCT_RULES.md`, `eng-principles-universal.md`, and `eng-principles-agentic.md` mirror the shape of their source principles documents, not this anatomy.
56	
57	## Meta-pack skills
58	
59	Skills with `pack: meta` are navigation and orientation artefacts, not workflow skills. They are exempt from: `principles_implemented` frontmatter, `predecessor` frontmatter, and the body sections Inputs, Outputs, Artefact template, Common rationalisations, Red flags, and Verification / exit criteria. They must still have: Title, Purpose, When to use, When not to use, and a References or equivalent lookup table.
60	
61	## See also
62	
63	- `docs/hook-anatomy.md` — for hooks
64	- `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` — for sub-agent personas
65	- `docs/brief-template.md` — for the brief that drives authoring
66	- `docs/authoring-learnings.md` — calibration guidance and failure modes
67	
```

66	
67	# Design doc
68	
69	## Purpose
70	
71	This skill produces a structured design document before any non-trivial implementation begins. It is the operational form of universal Principle 2 (design starts with the problem, not the stack): the seven-section structure mandated by Rule A2 makes solution-first approaches structurally incomplete. An agent or engineer cannot finish the artefact without working through Problem, Context, Constraints, Alternatives, and Operability in that order. The skill governs when a design doc is required (Rule A1), what structure it must follow (Rule A2), and which sections are hard rejections when absent (Rule A6). The deliverable is the document reviewers evaluate before any code is written — not the code itself.
72	
73	## When to use
74	
75	At least one Rule A1 trigger must fire. Any single trigger is sufficient:
76	
77	1. The work will take more than four weeks of engineering time.
78	2. The capability will be used by parties outside the team building it.
79	3. The change has meaningful impact on users, operational cost, or compliance posture.
80	
81	If a trigger is ambiguous, default to writing the doc. A short, fast doc costs less than a build that starts on a misunderstood problem.
82	
83	## When not to use
84	
85	- **None of the three triggers fire** — write a short ADR instead (Rule A3). A design doc for sub-threshold work is overhead; an ADR is the right fit.
86	- **The problem is not yet understood** — run a `spike` first, then return here. Writing a design doc for an undefined problem produces a document about the solution you assumed, not the problem you have.
87	- **Change is purely additive, clearly reversible, and has no shared-interface surface** — proceed directly to implementation. Not every shipped line needs a design doc.
88	
89	## Inputs
90	
91	- A problem description or feature request, however rough
92	- Named stakeholders and affected users
93	- Known constraints — functional and non-functional
94	- A rough scope estimate that suggests at least one Rule A1 trigger fires
95	- Prior design docs and ADRs for related capabilities (loaded per Agentic P1)
96	- Access to `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` (Rules A1, A2, A4, A5, A6, B2, B3)
97	
98	## Outputs
99	
100	Artefact path: `docs/design-docs/<kebab-case-name>/design-doc.md`
101	
102	The output is a complete design doc following the Rule A2 structure. Every section is present. The Operability section is fully populated. The document is ready for review before implementation begins.
103	
104	## Workflow
105	
106	**Step 1 — Trigger check [GATE]**
107	Verify at least one Rule A1 condition holds. State which one. If none holds, stop — recommend an ADR. If the problem itself is not yet understood, stop — recommend a spike. Do not proceed past this gate without naming the trigger condition.
108	
109	**Step 2 — Context load**
110	Load prior design docs and ADRs for related work. Load `eng-principles-universal.md` Rules A1, A2, A4, A5, A6, B2, B3. Load `eng-principles-agentic.md` P1, P3. Name each artefact loaded. This step is the operational form of Agentic P1: context is the asset; loading it is the work.
111	
112	**Step 3 — Problem section [GATE]**
113	Draft the Problem section first. No solution language appears until this section is complete. The problem statement names the affected users, describes current behaviour, and states the desired behaviour. Each of those three elements must be present before proceeding. A problem statement that slides into solution language ("users can't do X, so we should build Y") is sent back for rewrite. Proceed only when the problem is stated in problem terms.
114	
115	**Step 4 — Context and constraints**
116	Document background, history, and related prior decisions. Link relevant ADRs. State what has been tried before. State any inherited constraints. Then list non-functional requirements with numbers and units (Rule A4): latency at p95, availability SLO, throughput target, cost envelope, error budget, security posture. For each NFR, name its fitness function — the automated check that will verify it continuously (Rule A5). "Fast," "reliable," and "scalable" are not NFRs.
117	
118	**Step 5 — Alternatives**
119	List at least two alternatives, including the "do nothing" option. For each: a description, the blast radius if this option turns out to be wrong, and the reversal cost (Rule B3). One approach listed is an incomplete design doc. If you cannot describe what is wrong with the alternatives, you cannot explain why the recommended approach is right.
120	
121	**Step 6 — Recommended approach**
122	State the recommendation. Explain why it beats the named alternatives on the named constraints. This section comes after alternatives because the problem shapes the recommendation — not the reverse.
123	
124	**Step 7 — Consequences**
125	Name positive and negative consequences. Explicitly state whether a walking skeleton should precede the full build (Rule B2). A walking skeleton is the minimal end-to-end implementation — one request path wired through every real component in the real deployment environment. Pre-skeleton estimates are uncalibrated. If the path to production is already clear and integration risks are fully known, note that explicitly.
126	
127	**Step 8 — Operability plan [GATE]**
128	Complete every sub-field: metrics (what to graph), structured logs (what to emit), traces (spans to instrument), alerts (thresholds and on-call routing), rollback plan (steps and time estimate), capacity headroom, known failure modes with mitigations, upstream and downstream dependency failure modes. A skeletal or absent Operability section means the doc is not ready for review. This gate does not pass until every sub-field has content. This section is authored with the people who will operate the system — not written in isolation and delivered to them later (Rule A6).
129	
130	**Step 9 — Open questions**
131	List every unresolved question. Each question has an owner and a resolution deadline. An open questions section with no owners or dates is not an open questions section; it is a list of known unknowns being politely ignored.
132	
133	**Step 10 — Review hand-off**
134	Share the completed document for review before any implementation begins. Implementation begins only after the design doc is accepted. Sharing a draft with known incomplete sections is not a hand-off; it is a draft in progress.
135	
136	## Artefact template
137	
138	```markdown
139	---
140	name:
141	status: draft | in-review | accepted | superseded
142	authors:
143	created:
144	last_updated:
145	supersedes: <path to prior doc, or "none">
146	---
147	
148	# <title>
149	
150	## Problem
151	
152	<!-- What is wrong or missing? Who is affected, and how? What does current
153	     behaviour look like vs. desired behaviour? No solution language here. -->
154	
155	## Context
156	
157	<!-- Background, history, related prior decisions (link ADRs). What has been
158	     tried before? What constraints exist that were inherited rather than chosen? -->
159	
160	## Constraints
161	
162	<!-- Functional requirements and non-functional requirements. NFRs must have
163	     numbers: latency at p95, availability SLO, throughput, cost envelope.
164	     Each NFR names its fitness function — the automated check that will
165	     verify it continuously. -->
166	
167	## Alternatives considered
168	
169	<!-- At least two alternatives, including "do nothing." For each: description,
170	     blast radius if wrong, reversal cost. Only the chosen approach listed = incomplete. -->
171	
172	## Recommended approach
173	
174	<!-- The proposed solution. Why it wins on the named constraints. Comes after
175	     alternatives because the problem shapes the recommendation. -->
176	
177	## Consequences
178	
179	<!-- Positive and negative. State whether a walking skeleton should precede
180	     the full build and why. If integration risks are fully known, say so explicitly. -->
181	
182	## Operability plan
183	
184	<!-- Metrics, structured logs, traces, alerts (thresholds and routing), rollback
185	     plan (steps and time estimate), capacity headroom, known failure modes with
186	     mitigations, upstream and downstream dependency failure modes.
187	     Required. Absent = reject. -->
188	
189	## Open questions
190	
191	<!-- Unresolved questions. Each has an owner and a resolution deadline. -->
192	```
193	
194	## Common rationalisations
195	
196	| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |
197	|---|---|
198	| "It's too small to need a doc — we'll figure it out in the code." | Check Rule A1. If any trigger fires, the doc comes first. "Figuring it out in the code" is the build trap the trigger conditions were designed to catch. If no trigger fires, write an ADR. |
199	| "We already know what to build — the doc is just overhead." | The Alternatives and Constraints sections surface what you don't know you don't know. A design doc with only the chosen solution is incomplete by Rule A2. If the outcome is truly obvious, the doc will be short and the overhead will be low. |
200	| "We can write the doc after we understand the design better." | The design doc is how you understand the design better. Problem-first structure means the document shapes understanding — it is not a record of understanding already achieved. Post-hoc docs rationalise; they don't evaluate. |
201	| "The Operability section can be filled in closer to launch." | Rule A6 is explicit: the operability plan exists before the build begins, authored with the people who will operate it. "Add it later" means it never gets the scrutiny it needs before the decisions that make it hard are already made. |
202	| "We don't have enough alternatives to fill that section." | "Do nothing" is always an alternative. If you cannot describe what is wrong with the alternatives, you cannot explain why the recommended approach is right. A design doc that skips alternatives is not a design doc; it is an announcement. |
203	
204	## Red flags
205	
206	- Problem section contains solution language ("we will build…", "using X we can…")
207	- NFRs use adjectives instead of numbers ("fast," "highly available," "scalable")
208	- Alternatives section lists only the chosen approach
209	- Operability section is absent, has placeholder text, or is a stub
210	- No fitness function named for any NFR
211	- Walking skeleton not addressed in the Consequences section
212	- Open questions have no owners or no deadlines
213	- Document shared for review while known sections are incomplete
214	- Implementation has started before the doc is accepted
215	
216	## Verification / exit criteria
217	
218	The design doc passes review when:
219	
220	- At least one Rule A1 trigger is named and confirmed
221	- All Rule A2 sections are present and populated
222	- Every NFR has a number, a unit, and a named fitness function (Rules A4, A5)
223	- Alternatives section contains at least two options including "do nothing," each with blast radius and reversal cost (Rule B3)
224	- Operability plan covers all required sub-fields (Rule A6)
225	- Walking skeleton recommendation is addressed in the Consequences section (Rule B2)
226	- Every open question has an owner and a resolution deadline
227	- No implementation work has started before acceptance
228	
229	## References
230	
231	- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — P2, Rule A1, A2, A4, A5, A6, B2, B3
232	- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P1, P3, P5
233	- Larson, Will — "Staff Engineer" (trigger conditions for design docs)
234	- Nygard, Michael — "Release It!" (operability; ADR format)
235	- Cockburn, Alistair — walking skeleton (Rule B2 source)
236	- Ford, Parsons, Kua — "Building Evolutionary Architectures" (fitness functions; Rule A5 source)
237	- `references/nfr-categories.md` — NFR taxonomy for Rule A4
238	
```

45	
46	# Planning and task breakdown
47	
48	## Purpose
49	
50	This skill takes an accepted design doc and produces an ordered task list where each task is small enough to complete and verify independently. It bridges the gap between "we have an accepted design" and "we know exactly what to build in what order." The output is a sequence of well-scoped tasks, each with acceptance criteria, that whichever build skill the team uses will pick up one at a time.
51	
52	The goal is not comprehensiveness — it is clarity and verifiability. A task list that looks thorough but whose tasks are too big to verify will not survive contact with implementation. Every task must answer: "how do I know this is done?"
53	
54	## When to use
55	
56	- A design doc has been accepted and implementation is ready to begin.
57	- A well-scoped spec exists for a change that doesn't meet the Rule A1 design-doc trigger, but is complex enough that sequencing and acceptance criteria are non-obvious.
58	
59	## When not to use
60	
61	- **No accepted design doc and the work exceeds a Rule A1 trigger** — run `design-doc` first. A draft or in-flight doc does not count as accepted.
62	- **The scope is unclear or contested** — resolve scope ambiguity in the design doc before breaking down tasks. Task breakdown from a vague spec produces tasks nobody believes in.
63	
64	## Inputs
65	
66	An accepted design doc covering at minimum: the problem being solved, the chosen approach, the components being changed or created, and the constraints. The design doc does not need to be perfect; it needs to be stable enough that the approach won't change during implementation.
67	
68	## Outputs
69	
70	An ordered task list at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`. Each task has: a one-sentence description, acceptance criteria (how you know it's done), and any upstream dependencies called out. The list begins with the walking skeleton as task 1.
71	
72	## Workflow
73	
74	**1. Read the design doc end-to-end.**
75	Do not start decomposing until you have read the full document. The most common task breakdown error is starting with the first section and ignoring the constraints and operability sections at the end. Those sections often constrain the sequencing.
76	
77	**2. Identify the walking skeleton.**
78	The walking skeleton is the thinnest possible slice that exercises the full path end-to-end — from input to output — without filling in the real functionality. It is always task 1. If you cannot identify a walking skeleton (a slice that makes the system run, however crudely), the design doc is missing something. Stop and resolve it before proceeding.
79	
80	Examples:
81	- API endpoint: a handler that accepts the request, returns a hardcoded response, passes a smoke test — no real logic yet.
82	- UI feature: a route that renders the page with static content — no data binding yet.
83	- Pipeline stage: a step that accepts the input format and emits the output format with placeholder data — no processing yet.
84	
85	**3. Identify the feature slices.**
86	Starting from the walking skeleton, list the increments that build toward the full design. Each increment should:
87	- Extend functionality in one direction (add real logic, add a field, add an error path)
88	- Be independently testable
89	- Leave the system in a working, deployable state
90	
91	Do not batch unrelated concerns into one slice. If a task touches the data layer AND the API AND the UI, it is three tasks.
92	
93	**4. [GATE] Write acceptance criteria for each task.**
94	Before ordering tasks, write the "done" check for each one. An acceptance criterion is a test, an assertion, or a measurable condition — not a description of what to build. If you cannot write the acceptance criterion, the task is not well enough understood to schedule.
95	
96	Format: `Task is done when: [specific, verifiable condition]`
97	
98	- Bad: "Task is done when the search feature is implemented."
99	- Good: "Task is done when: a query against the `/search` endpoint returns a 200 with the correct result shape for a known test input, covered by an automated integration test."
100	
101	**5. [GATE] Size check.**
102	Review every task. Any task that:
103	- Cannot be described in one sentence
104	- Has more than one acceptance criterion that requires a separate deployment to verify
105	- Would take more than one working session (~4–6 hours) to implement and verify
106	
107	is too big. Split it. Size is not about effort estimation; it is about verification granularity. Small tasks surface problems early.
108	
109	Example — too big:
110	> "Implement user authentication, including sign-up, login, password reset, and session management."
111	
112	Split into verifiable tasks:
113	> Task A: POST /auth/signup creates a user and returns a JWT — covered by an integration test.
114	> Task B: POST /auth/login returns a JWT for valid credentials, 401 for invalid — covered by tests.
115	> Task C: POST /auth/password-reset sends a reset email — covered by a test mocking the mailer.
116	> Task D: Session expiry revokes access on subsequent requests — covered by a test advancing time.
117	
118	Each task has one observable outcome, one test, and one commit boundary. That is a correctly sized task.
119	
120	**6. Order by dependency.**
121	Sequence tasks so that each one's dependencies are complete before it starts. Call out explicit dependencies (on other tasks in this list, on external work, on platform capabilities). If a task depends on work from another team, flag it immediately — Rule C4: dependencies are surfaced before commitment.
122	
123	**7. Write and file the task list.**
124	Use the template below. File at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`.
125	
126	## Task list template
127	
128	```markdown
129	# Tasks: <feature-slug>
130	
131	Design doc: docs/designs/<feature-slug>.md
132	Last updated: <date>
133	
134	## Task list
135	
136	### Task 1 — Walking skeleton
137	**Description:** [one sentence — what does the skeleton do?]
138	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
139	**Dependencies:** none
140	
141	### Task 2 — [name]
142	**Description:** [one sentence]
143	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
144	**Dependencies:** Task 1
145	
146	### Task N — [name]
147	**Description:** [one sentence]
148	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
149	**Dependencies:** [list, or "none"]
150	
151	## Open questions
152	<!-- Anything blocking or uncertain that was surfaced during breakdown.
153	     Do not start implementation until these are resolved. -->
154	```
155	
156	## Common rationalisations
157	
158	| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |
159	|---|---|
160	| "The tasks are obvious from the design doc — no need to write them out." | If they're obvious, writing them out takes 15 minutes. If it takes more, they weren't as obvious as assumed. The acceptance criteria are worth the time even if the tasks aren't. |
161	| "We'll figure out the order as we go." | Unordered tasks produce surprise dependencies mid-implementation. Dependency discovery during a build is expensive; discovery before the build is cheap. |
162	| "This task is a bit big but we know what we're doing." | Task size is not a trust issue — it is a verification issue. Big tasks fail silently. Small tasks fail loudly and early. |
163	| "The design doc isn't fully accepted but it's close enough." | An almost-accepted design doc means the approach is still negotiable. Tasks broken down from a negotiable design are likely to be redone. |
164	| "We don't need a walking skeleton — we'll integrate at the end." | Integration at the end is the most expensive integration there is. The walking skeleton exists to find integration problems on day 1, not day 29. |
165	| "We already know the dependencies — no need to list them." | Dependencies change during implementation; listing them before forces you to verify each one rather than discover them when they block you. A listed dependency that turns out not to exist costs nothing. An unlisted dependency that surfaces mid-sprint costs days. |
166	| "I'll write acceptance criteria after I implement so I know what 'done' looks like." | By then you've already built the wrong thing. Acceptance criteria written before implementation are a spec — they constrain what you build. Criteria written after implementation describe what you built. The goal is the former. |
167	| "The task list will need to change anyway, so there's no point spending time on it." | The value is not the list surviving implementation intact. It is the thinking done while creating it — discovering the dependencies, sizing the slices, identifying the walking skeleton. A plan that gets revised is still better than no plan. |
168	
169	## Red flags
170	
171	- The walking skeleton is missing or is listed as something other than task 1.
172	- Any task has more than one independent acceptance criterion.
173	- Acceptance criteria say "feature is implemented" or "code is written" rather than describing a verifiable condition.
174	- Acceptance criteria reference implementation details ("the function is written") rather than observable behaviour ("the endpoint returns X for input Y").
175	- The task list was written before reading the constraints section of the design doc.
176	- A task depends on unresolved external work that hasn't been flagged.
177	- The list was generated from the design doc introduction without reading the full document.
178	- A "setup" or "scaffolding" task appears before the walking skeleton, deferring integration discovery.
179	
180	## Verification / exit criteria
181	
182	The skill has run correctly when:
183	
184	1. A task list exists at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`.
185	2. Task 1 is the walking skeleton, with an acceptance criterion that can be checked independently of downstream tasks.
186	3. Every task has a single, concrete acceptance criterion stating a verifiable condition.
187	4. Every task can be described in one sentence.
188	5. Dependencies are listed for every task that has them.
189	6. Any external or cross-team dependency has been surfaced explicitly in the open questions section.
190	7. No "setup" or "scaffolding" task precedes the walking skeleton — if infrastructure is needed before the skeleton, the skeleton task encompasses it.
191	8. The open questions section is either populated with owners and resolution dates, or explicitly marked empty because no unresolved questions remain.
192	
193	## References
194	
195	- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — Rule A1 (design doc trigger), Rule B1 (YAGNI per slice), Rule B2 (walking skeleton first), Rule C4 (dependencies before commitment)
196	- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P3 (no drift from design doc)
197	- `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — the upstream output that this skill consumes
198	- Ryan Singer — "Shape Up" (appetite and scope as planning primitives)
199	
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/pde-skills/skills/render-html/SKILL.md",
  "content": "---\nname: render-html\ndescription: >\n  Convert a markdown design doc, plan, ADR, or roadmap into a single self-contained\n  HTML file for human review. Use when an artefact is about to go to review or\n  sign-off and the reviewer experience matters — markdown flattens spatial content\n  (alternatives, dependency graphs, diffs, timelines) that HTML can present\n  faithfully. Trigger phrases: \"render this doc as HTML\", \"make this reviewable\",\n  \"convert design doc to HTML\", \"I want to share this plan\", \"share this for\n  review\", \"make a readable version\". Use this skill whenever the user is about to\n  hand a markdown artefact to a human reviewer, even if they don't explicitly say\n  the word HTML.\npack: engineering\nlifecycle_stage: review\nprinciples_implemented:\n  - source: eng-agentic\n    id: P7\n    bucket: standalone\n  - source: eng-universal\n    id: P1\n    bucket: standalone\nlength_target: 180-240\nauthor: Anton Babushkin\npredecessor:\n  repo: none\n  skill: none\n  relation: new\nkept_from_predecessor: n/a\nchanged_from_predecessor: n/a\n---\n\n# Render HTML\n\n## Purpose\n\nThis skill converts a markdown artefact — design doc, task list, ADR, roadmap, postmortem — into one self-contained HTML file written next to the source as `<source>.html`. The deliverable is for humans about to review or sign off on the artefact, not for archival or publication. It is the operational form of agentic Principle 7 (memory lives in artefacts): the doc itself, made navigable and legible, is the persistent record reviewers and future agents read. And it is a lever on universal Principle 1 (shipped is not done; observed is done): better review UX raises the rate at which review actually happens, which raises the rate at which outcomes get observed.\n\nThe v0.1 skill produces a clean walking-skeleton render — title, sticky TOC sidebar generated from H2/H3, prose body, code blocks, footer with provenance. Rich pattern-specific treatments (side-by-side alternatives, kanban roadmaps, diff blocks, dependency timelines) arrive in later iterations as the pattern catalogue is built out. The walking skeleton must work end-to-end on any structured markdown before any pattern logic is added.\n\n## When to use\n\n- The user asks to \"render\", \"convert to HTML\", \"make this reviewable\", or \"share this doc\" and points at a markdown file.\n- A markdown artefact is about to go to a human reviewer for design review, code review prep, ADR sign-off, or stakeholder alignment.\n- The doc is long enough that navigation matters (more than ~3 H2 sections) or contains structure that flattens badly in markdown previewers.\n\n## When not to use\n\n- **Drafting** — write the markdown first. HTML rendering is for finished or near-finished artefacts, not works in progress. The output is regenerated each time the source changes; rendering mid-draft burns cycles for no review benefit.\n- **Single-screen documents** — a 30-line note doesn't earn the overhead of a TOC and shell. Send the markdown.\n- **Documents the reviewer will edit** — HTML round-trip back to markdown is not supported. If reviewers want to suggest edits, send the markdown or use `/crit`.\n- **Multi-document sites** — one source in, one file out. For a site, use a static-site generator.\n\n## Inputs\n\n- Path to a single `.md` file with at least one H1 and structured H2/H3 sections.\n- Optional: prior `<source>.html` exists alongside the source (triggers overwrite confirmation).\n\n## Outputs\n\n- A single HTML file written to `<source>.html` — same directory as the source, same basename, `.html` extension.\n- The file is self-contained: inline `<style>`, inline `<script>`, no `<link rel=\"stylesheet\">`, no `<script src=>`, no remote images. Opens correctly via `file://` with the network offline.\n- Size budget: ≤ ~300KB for typical inputs; hard cap 500KB.\n\n## Workflow\n\n**Step 1 — Source check [GATE]**\nRead the source markdown. Confirm it has at least one H1 and at least one H2. If not, stop and report: \"render-html expects structured markdown — source has no H1/H2.\" Do not invent structure to make the doc renderable. The skill is a renderer, not a restructurer.\n\n**Step 2 — Overwrite check [GATE]**\nCompute the output path: replace the source's `.md` extension with `.html`. If a file already exists at that path, prompt the user for confirmation before proceeding: \"`<output-path>` exists. Overwrite? (y/n)\". Do not silently overwrite. Do not append timestamps or version suffixes — re-rendering must be idempotent so diffs are meaningful. If the user declines, stop.\n\n**Step 3 — Inventory headings**\nWalk the markdown and collect every H2 and H3 in document order. Generate a stable slug for each (kebab-case from the heading text, deduplicated with `-2`, `-3` if needed). These become both anchor IDs in the body and the TOC entries in the sidebar. H1 is the document title — it goes in the page header, not the TOC.\n\n**Step 4 — Convert body**\nRender the markdown body using standard CommonMark semantics: H2/H3 with anchor IDs from Step 3, paragraphs, lists, blockquotes, fenced code blocks (preserving language hints), inline code, tables, links. For v0.1 do not apply pattern-specific treatments — every section renders as straightforward prose. Pattern detection lands in a later iteration.\n\n**Step 5 — Compose the page**\nWrap the body in the inline HTML shell (template below). Inject the TOC from Step 3 into the sidebar. Inject the document title into the header. Inject a provenance footer with the source path and render date.\n\n**Step 6 — Self-containment check [GATE]**\nBefore writing the file, verify the composed HTML contains zero `<link rel=\"stylesheet\">`, zero `<script src=>`, and no `<img src=\"http...\">` or `<img src=\"//...\">`. SVG and base64 data URIs are allowed; remote URLs are not. If any external reference is present, fix the shell rather than ship the violation.\n\n**Step 7 — Write and report**\nWrite the file to `<source>.html`. Report the output path, file size, and the count of H2/H3 entries in the TOC. Tell the user: \"Open the file in your browser to verify TOC navigation and that no console errors appear.\" This handoff is required — the skill cannot observe the rendered page itself (universal Principle 1: the agent has shipped, the user observes).\n\n## Artefact template\n\nThe HTML shell injected in Step 5. Tokens in `{{double-braces}}` are substituted by the skill.\n\n```html\n<!doctype html>\n<html lang=\"en\">\n<head>\n<meta charset=\"utf-8\">\n<meta name=\"viewport\" content=\"width=device-width, initial-scale=1\">\n<title>{{title}}</title>\n<style>\n  :root { --fg: #111; --muted: #666; --bg: #fff; --rule: #e5e5e5; --accent: #0a58ca; }\n  @media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) {\n    :root { --fg: #eee; --muted: #999; --bg: #111; --rule: #2a2a2a; --accent: #6ea8fe; }\n  }\n  html, body { margin: 0; padding: 0; background: var(--bg); color: var(--fg); }\n  body { font: 16px/1.55 ui-sans-serif, system-ui, -apple-system, \"Segoe UI\", Roboto, sans-serif; }\n  .layout { display: grid; grid-template-columns: 260px 1fr; max-width: 1100px; margin: 0 auto; }\n  nav.toc { position: sticky; top: 0; align-self: start; max-height: 100vh; overflow-y: auto; padding: 24px 16px; border-right: 1px solid var(--rule); font-size: 14px; }\n  nav.toc h2 { font-size: 12px; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.05em; color: var(--muted); margin: 0 0 8px; }\n  nav.toc ul { list-style: none; padding: 0; margin: 0; }\n  nav.toc li { margin: 4px 0; }\n  nav.toc li.h3 { padding-left: 12px; font-size: 13px; }\n  nav.toc a { color: var(--fg); text-decoration: none; }\n  nav.toc a:hover, nav.toc a.active { color: var(--accent); }\n  main { padding: 32px 40px; max-width: 72ch; }\n  main h1 { font-size: 28px; margin: 0 0 4px; }\n  main .meta { color: var(--muted); font-size: 13px; margin-bottom: 32px; }\n  main h2 { font-size: 22px; margin-top: 40px; padding-top: 8px; border-top: 1px solid var(--rule); }\n  main h3 { font-size: 17px; margin-top: 24px; }\n  main pre { background: rgba(127,127,127,0.08); padding: 12px 14px; border-radius: 6px; overflow-x: auto; font-size: 13px; }\n  main code { font-family: ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, monospace; font-size: 0.92em; }\n  main table { border-collapse: collapse; margin: 12px 0; }\n  main th, main td { border: 1px solid var(--rule); padding: 6px 10px; text-align: left; }\n  footer { grid-column: 1 / -1; padding: 24px 40px; color: var(--muted); font-size: 12px; border-top: 1px solid var(--rule); }\n  @media print {\n    nav.toc { display: none; }\n    .layout { grid-template-columns: 1fr; }\n  }\n  @media (max-width: 720px) {\n    .layout { grid-template-columns: 1fr; }\n    nav.toc { position: static; max-height: none; border-right: none; border-bottom: 1px solid var(--rule); }\n  }\n</style>\n</head>\n<body>\n<div class=\"layout\">\n<nav class=\"toc\" aria-label=\"Table of contents\">\n<h2>Contents</h2>\n<ul>\n  {{toc-entries: <li class=\"h2\"><a href=\"#slug\">Heading</a></li> ...}}\n</ul>\n</nav>\n<main>\n<h1>{{title}}</h1>\n<p class=\"meta\">Rendered from <code>{{source-path}}</code> on {{render-date}}</p>\n{{body-html}}\n</main>\n<footer>Generated by render-html. Re-render the source markdown to update.</footer>\n</div>\n<script>\n  // Scrollspy: highlight the TOC entry for the section currently in view.\n  const links = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('nav.toc a'));\n  const targets = links.map(a => document.getElementById(a.getAttribute('href').slice(1))).filter(Boolean);\n  const byId = new Map(targets.map((el, i) => [el.id, links[i]]));\n  const io = new IntersectionObserver(entries => {\n    for (const e of entries) {\n      if (e.isIntersecting) {\n        links.forEach(a => a.classList.remove('active'));\n        byId.get(e.target.id)?.classList.add('active');\n      }\n    }\n  }, { rootMargin: '-30% 0px -60% 0px' });\n  targets.forEach(t => io.observe(t));\n</script>\n</body>\n</html>\n```\n\n## Common rationalisations\n\n| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |\n|---|---|\n| \"The markdown previewer is fine — they can just read it on GitHub.\" | GitHub flattens side-by-side alternatives into stacked blocks, hides long task lists in scroll, and gives no in-doc navigation for 40-section design docs. The reviewer experience is the bottleneck on outcome-observation (universal Principle 1); a renderer optimised for review removes it. |\n| \"I'll add a CDN link for the syntax highlighter / icon font / chart library.\" | The self-containment rule is load-bearing. Reviewers open these files via `file://`, attach them to emails, store them in private wikis. A broken CDN reference produces a silently-degraded page that the reviewer doesn't know to distrust. Inline or omit. |\n| \"Let me append a timestamp to the filename so I don't overwrite.\" | Idempotent re-rendering is the property that makes the output diffable and the source the canonical record. Versioned filenames produce orphaned copies. Prompt before overwriting (Step 2); do not invent new names. |\n| \"The source has no headings yet — let me invent some structure to make it renderable.\" | The skill is a renderer, not a restructurer. Inventing headings creates artefacts that look authoritative but were never agreed. If the source isn't structured, fix the source first. |\n| \"I'll skip the TOC for short docs to save space.\" | The TOC is the navigation contract reviewers learn to expect. Suppressing it inconsistently trains reviewers to scroll-hunt every doc. If a doc is short enough that the TOC is useless, the doc is short enough that the skill doesn't apply (see When not to use). |\n\n## Red flags\n\n- Output contains `<link rel=\"stylesheet\">`, `<script src=>`, or remote `<img>` URLs.\n- File written to a path other than `<source>.html` (no timestamps, no version suffixes, no other directories).\n- Existing output silently overwritten without prompt.\n- TOC entries do not match the H2/H3 headings in the body.\n- Body markdown silently dropped (e.g., a fenced code block missing) rather than rendered.\n- File size above 500KB.\n- HTML claims to render structure not present in the source.\n\n## Verification / exit criteria\n\nThe skill has run correctly when:\n\n1. `<source>.html` exists in the same directory as the source.\n2. Opening the file via `file://` in a browser produces no console errors or warnings.\n3. The DevTools Network panel shows zero network requests after load.\n4. Every H2 and H3 in the source appears as a TOC entry and is reachable by clicking.\n5. The page renders correctly with the network disabled.\n6. File size is below 500KB.\n7. Re-running the skill against the same source (after declining overwrite) leaves the existing file untouched; accepting overwrite produces an HTML whose diff against the previous render is limited to the provenance line.\n8. The user has been told to open the file and confirm — the skill has not declared the render verified on its own (universal Principle 1).\n\n## References\n\n- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P7 (memory lives in artefacts)\n- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — P1 (shipped is not done; observed is done)\n- Thariq Shihipar — \"HTML effectiveness\" (https://thariqs.github.io/html-effectiveness/) — case for HTML as a substrate for spatial review content\n- `docs/skill-anatomy.md` — frontmatter and section-order contract\n- `docs/tasks/render-html.md` — task plan; later iterations extract the shell to `references/html-skeleton.md` and build the pattern catalogue at `references/html-patterns.md`\n"
}
```
