1	---
2	id: ADR-engagement-modifier-constants
3	status: accepted
4	date: 2026-05-14
5	authors: Anton Babushkin
6	linear: ABA-66
7	supersedes: none
8	---
9	
10	# ADR — Engagement-modifier constants (pre-registered)
11	
12	## Status
13	
14	**Accepted.** Pre-registered before the Task 6 backtest is run, so the backtest cannot be tuned to its own answer. One in-sample revision is permitted (recorded as a superseding ADR with explicit "in-sample retune" rationale); a second failure ships the modifier disabled-by-default per the design-doc Week-2 → Week-3 gate.
15	
16	## Context
17	
18	`/stock-model` is being extended with an engagement-KPI–driven base-IV modifier (design doc: `docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/design-doc.md`, ABA-66). The modifier reads the latest as-published engagement KPI (e.g. META DAP, RDDT DAUq) from EDGAR 8-K Ex 99.1, classifies the YoY trend into a direction × magnitude band, and perturbs the Year-1 revenue anchor by a small bounded multiplier. The full effect is then clamped against an output cap on base IV.
19	
20	The numerical constants that govern this pipeline have to be fixed **before** the Task 6 backtest runs. Otherwise the backtest's hit rate becomes a function of constant tuning, and NFR7 (≥60% revision-direction agreement) collapses into circular validation. This ADR records each constant, the alternative considered, and the data point that would prompt a revision.
21	
22	Tasks 1, 3, and 4 (walking-skeleton spike, reproducibility harness, backtest data-source feasibility) are complete and inform the choices below.
23	
24	## Decision
25	
26	The following constants are pre-registered for the Task 6 backtest and for the eventual production wiring (Tasks 8–14):
27	
28	### C1 — Deadband: ±2% YoY
29	
30	If `abs(yoy_change) < 0.02`, set `direction = 0`, `magnitude = "neutral"`, `base_anchor_multiplier = 1.00` (no-op).
31	
32	**Reasoning.** Reported YoY KPI deltas in the seed-ticker set cluster tightly around their long-run trend (META DAP YoY oscillates 3–8% quarter-over-quarter; RDDT DAUq 15–25%). A 2% deadband filters reporting-noise-tier changes (rounding in stated KPI, mix-shift artefacts) without absorbing meaningful inflections. The two spike samples (META +4%, RDDT +17%) both sit well outside the deadband, confirming the threshold doesn't reject the actual signal.
33	
34	**Alternative considered.** ±1% (tighter; risks reading rounding as signal) and ±3% (looser; risks absorbing the bottom edge of meaningful META decel). Both rejected as worse on the seed set.
35	
36	**Revision trigger.** If backtest direction-agreement is <60% AND ≥30% of misses occur on |YoY|<3% samples, the deadband may be widened to ±3% as the single permitted in-sample retune.
37	
38	### C2 — Strong-magnitude threshold: 8% YoY
39	
40	If `abs(yoy_change) >= 0.08`, `magnitude = "strong"`; otherwise `magnitude = "mild"`.
41	
42	**Reasoning.** 8% separates META's normal DAP cadence (mid-single-digit YoY) from clearly out-of-trend prints (≥8% accel or decel signals a regime change). RDDT's print cadence is structurally higher (mid-teens to mid-twenties), so RDDT prints land in `strong` by default — which matches the intuition: a high-growth app's signal is louder per unit of YoY-change. Spike samples (META 4% → mild; RDDT 17% → strong) match the intended classification.
43	
44	**Alternative considered.** Per-ticker thresholds (calibrated to each ticker's baseline volatility). Rejected for v1 because (a) it doubles the surface area to tune without backtest evidence supporting the lift, and (b) the global threshold's failure mode is well-defined — it under-weights large-cap signals and over-weights high-growth signals. OQ2 in the design doc explicitly defers per-ticker calibration to post-backtest evidence.
45	
46	**Revision trigger.** If backtest shows direction-agreement is materially asymmetric between mild and strong samples (e.g. strong agrees ≥70%, mild agrees ≤50%), this is the prompt to move to per-ticker thresholds — but that revision creates a *new* ADR with fresh data, not an in-sample retune of this one.
47	
48	### C3 — Input cap: ±4% on Year-1 revenue anchor
49	
50	`base_anchor_multiplier = 1 + direction × (0.02 if mild else 0.04)` → ∈ [0.96, 1.04].
51	
52	**Reasoning.** ±4% caps the modifier's input perturbation at a level smaller than the typical quarter-to-quarter analyst-consensus revision (which empirically runs ±2–6% on the seed set). The modifier should nudge the Y1 anchor in the direction of where consensus is *about to revise*, not anticipate the revision's full magnitude. ±4% is the largest input perturbation that, combined with the C4 output cap, holds the design's claim that the modifier is a directional nudge rather than a re-forecast.
53	
54	**Alternative considered.** ±2% (too small — strong signals get the same nudge as mild ones; loses the magnitude band) and ±6% (too large — under leveraged DCF sensitivities the output cap C4 binds frequently, defeating the input cap's purpose). ±4% is the value where the input cap and the output cap are both occasionally binding, which is the desired regime.
55	
56	**Revision trigger.** If, post-Task 6, the input cap binds on >40% of samples while the output cap binds on <10%, the input cap is too tight; if the output cap binds on >30% while the input cap is rarely the active constraint, the input cap is too loose. Either case prompts re-authoring (new ADR).
57	
58	### C4 — Output cap: ≤5% on base IV
59	
60	Post-application, if `abs(base_iv_after / base_iv_before − 1) > 0.05`, clamp the multiplier downward until the IV impact is ≤5%, and record `status: "clamped"` with `clamped_from: <original_multiplier>` in the JSON.
61	
62	**Reasoning.** The modifier exists to nudge IV in the direction of expected consensus revision, not to dominate the valuation. A 5% IV ceiling means the modifier can never single-handedly flip a base-case verdict; it can only shade it. Combined with C3, this enforces two-level defense in depth: an input bound that's easy to reason about and an output bound that catches DCF leverage edge cases.
63	
64	**Alternative considered.** A single output cap with no input cap (simpler, but loses the auditable narrative — "we said the modifier was a ±4% nudge" reads better than "we said it was a ≤5% effect, derived"). Rejected for explainability reasons.
65	
66	**Revision trigger.** If `engagement_modifier_base_iv_impact_pct` regularly exceeds 5% pre-clamp (>20% of applied runs) the cap is doing real work — that's fine, no revision. If the cap *never* binds, C3 is conservative enough that C4 is dead weight, and a future ADR may simplify.
67	
68	### C5 — Base-scenario only
69	
70	The multiplier is applied to the **base** scenario's Y1 anchor only. Bull and bear scenarios are unaffected.
71	
72	**Reasoning.** Bull and bear scenarios have their own intentional bias by construction (optimistic / pessimistic growth assumptions). Compounding an additional engagement-direction nudge onto them double-counts: a positive engagement print already partially explains why the bull scenario is bull-shaped. The modifier targets the *central* estimate where the analyst-consensus-revision signal it tracks is most directly priced. This also preserves scenario-axis independence — the bull–base–bear spread is unchanged by the modifier (it shifts the central value, not the width).
73	
74	**Alternative considered.** Apply to all three scenarios (more dramatic, more confusing). Apply only to bear when KPI is negative / only to bull when KPI is positive (asymmetric — risk: looks like cherry-picking to reinforce conclusions). Both rejected. The base-only choice resolves OQ5 in the spike-outputs note.
75	
76	**Revision trigger.** If post-launch the user reports that bull/bear scenarios feel "stale" relative to base after a strong engagement print, the resolution is more likely a UI clarification (the spread shifts with base, since bull = base × multiplier and bear = base × multiplier) than an ADR change. Revisit only on direct evidence that scenario-axis independence is causing decision-confusion.
77	
78	### C6 — Backtest-pass threshold: 60% direction agreement on ≥24 valid samples
79	
80	NFR7 ships only if Task 6's revision-direction agreement is **≥60%** on **≥24 valid ticker-quarter samples**.
81	
82	**Reasoning.** The null hypothesis for direction agreement is 50% (coin-flip). For the modifier to be load-bearing it must demonstrate a meaningful lift above coin-flip. ≥10pp above 50% is the smallest lift where, on a 24-sample test, the result is unlikely to be noise (binomial p-value ≈ 0.27 for exactly 60% on n=24; tightens fast as n grows toward the 40-sample target). 60% is therefore: high enough to rule out coin-flip-with-noise, low enough that a single calibration miss doesn't sink an otherwise-real signal, and clear enough to be a falsifiable up-front commitment.
83	
84	**Alternative considered.** 55% (closer to noise floor; not enough lift to claim the modifier is doing work). 65–70% (too aggressive for v1; the seed-set sample is small and even a real signal might not clear it). Both rejected.
85	
86	**Revision trigger.** The threshold itself is not revisable in-sample. If Task 6 returns 55–59%, the design-doc Week-2 → Week-3 gate fires: one in-sample constant revision (most likely C1 or C2) is permitted, then the backtest re-runs against the same threshold. A second sub-60% result ships the modifier disabled-by-default with `--engagement-modifier` as opt-in.
87	
88	## Consequences
89	
90	**Positive.**
91	
92	- Task 6 backtest runs against fixed constants — its hit rate is an honest test of the underlying signal, not of constant tuning.
93	- The audit trail (`stages.model.engagement_modifier` JSON) records every constant the run used, so downstream replay produces identical output.
94	- The two-cap structure (C3 input + C4 output) is explainable to a reader in two sentences without requiring DCF intuition.
95	- Pre-registering 60% as the kill criterion makes the spike→build gate falsifiable on evidence rather than negotiation.
96	
97	**Negative.**
98	
99	- Global thresholds (C1, C2) under-fit the variance between META-class and RDDT-class signals. The design accepts this for v1 in exchange for not double-spending the in-sample revision budget on per-ticker calibration before the backtest has run.
100	- Base-only application (C5) means a strong engagement print does not visibly perturb the bull or bear scenarios — users who don't read the doc may miss why. Mitigation: the report UI surfaces the modifier in the base column with a tooltip noting the scenario-axis-independence rationale (Task 11/12 scope).
101	- One-revision budget creates pressure to "spend" it on the first sub-threshold result rather than investigate root cause. Mitigation: a revision requires explaining which constant moved and why, recorded as a superseding ADR.
102	
103	**Neutral / open.**
104	
105	- OQ1 (revenue vs. FCF-margin lever) is not resolved by this ADR. The Y1 anchor named throughout is the **revenue** anchor per the design-doc default. If Task 9 (live wiring) surfaces a reason to perturb the FCF-margin path instead, that is a new ADR.
106	
107	## Linkage
108	
109	- Design doc: `docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/design-doc.md` §Constraints (FR3, FR4), §Recommended approach §3.
110	- Spike outputs: `docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/spike-outputs.md` (META and RDDT classification samples).
111	- Feasibility note: `docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/backtest-data-source.md` (Task 4 — data-source path used by C6's backtest).
112	- Task plan: `docs/tasks/engagement-kpi-enrichment.md` (Task 5 = this ADR; Task 6 = backtest gated on it).
113	
```

border-left: 3px solid #6c757d; }\n  .conseq-block h4 { color: inherit; }\n</style>\n</head>\n<body>\n<div class=\"layout\">\n<nav class=\"toc\" aria-label=\"Table of contents\">\n<h2>Contents</h2>\n<ul>\n  <li class=\"h2\"><a href=\"#status\">Status</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h2\"><a href=\"#context\">Context</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h2\"><a href=\"#decision\">Decision</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h3\"><a href=\"#c1\">C1 — Deadband ±2%</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h3\"><a href=\"#c2\">C2 — Strong-magnitude 8%</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h3\"><a href=\"#c3\">C3 — Input cap ±4%</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h3\"><a href=\"#c4\">C4 — Output cap ≤5%</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h3\"><a href=\"#c5\">C5 — Base-scenario only</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h3\"><a href=\"#c6\">C6 — Backtest threshold</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h2\"><a href=\"#consequences\">Consequences</a></li>\n  <li class=\"h2\"><a href=\"#linkage\">Linkage</a></li>\n</ul>\n</nav>\n<main>\n<h1>ADR — Engagement-modifier constants (pre-registered)</h1>\n<p class=\"meta\">Rendered from <code>docs/adrs/engagement-modifier-constants.md</code> on 2026-05-17</p>\n<dl class=\"frontmatter\">\n  <dt>id</dt><dd>ADR-engagement-modifier-constants</dd>\n  <dt>status</dt><dd><span class=\"status-pill\">accepted</span></dd>\n  <dt>date</dt><dd>2026-05-14</dd>\n  <dt>authors</dt><dd>Anton Babushkin</dd>\n  <dt>linear</dt><dd>ABA-66</dd>\n  <dt>supersedes</dt><dd>none</dd>\n</dl>\n\n<h2 id=\"status\">Status</h2>\n<section class=\"p-callout p-callout-status\">\n  <header class=\"p-callout-head\">Status</header>\n  <div class=\"p-callout-body\">\n    <p><strong>Accepted.</strong> Pre-registered before the Task 6 backtest is run, so the backtest cannot be tuned to its own answer. One in-sample revision is permitted (recorded as a superseding ADR with explicit \"in-sample retune\" rationale); a second failure ships the modifier disabled-by-default per the design-doc Week-2 → Week-3 gate.</p>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<h2 id=\"context\">Context</h2>\n<section class=\"p-callout p-callout-context\">\n  <header class=\"p-callout-head\">Context</header>\n  <div class=\"p-callout-body\">\n    <p><code>/stock-model</code> is being extended with an engagement-KPI–driven base-IV modifier (design doc: <code>docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/design-doc.md</code>, ABA-66). The modifier reads the latest as-published engagement KPI (e.g. META DAP, RDDT DAUq) from EDGAR 8-K Ex 99.1, classifies the YoY trend into a direction × magnitude band, and perturbs the Year-1 revenue anchor by a small bounded multiplier. The full effect is then clamped against an output cap on base IV.</p>\n    <p>The numerical constants that govern this pipeline have to be fixed <strong>before</strong> the Task 6 backtest runs. Otherwise the backtest's hit rate becomes a function of constant tuning, and NFR7 (≥60% revision-direction agreement) collapses into circular validation. This ADR records each constant, the alternative considered, and the data point that would prompt a revision.</p>\n    <p>Tasks 1, 3, and 4 (walking-skeleton spike, reproducibility harness, backtest data-source feasibility) are complete and inform the choices below.</p>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<h2 id=\"decision\">Decision</h2>\n<section class=\"p-callout p-callout-decision\">\n  <header class=\"p-callout-head\">Decision</header>\n  <div class=\"p-callout-body\">\n    <p>The following constants are pre-registered for the Task 6 backtest and for the eventual production wiring (Tasks 8–14):</p>\n  </div>\n</section>\n\n<div class=\"const-card\" id=\"c1\">\n  <h3><span class=\"const-id\">C1</span> Deadband: ±2% YoY</h3>\n  <div class=\"const-formula\">If <code>abs(yoy_change) &lt; 0.02</code>, set <code>direction = 0</code>, <code>magnitude = \"neutral\"</code>, <code>base_anchor_multiplier = 1.00</code> (no-op).</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4>\n  <p>Reported YoY KPI deltas in the seed-ticker set cluster tightly around their long-run trend (META DAP YoY oscillates 3–8% quarter-over-quarter; RDDT DAUq 15–25%). A 2% deadband filters reporting-noise-tier changes (rounding in stated KPI, mix-shift artefacts) without absorbing meaningful inflections. The two spike samples (META +4%, RDDT +17%) both sit well outside the deadband, confirming the threshold doesn't reject the actual signal.</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4>\n  <p>±1% (tighter; risks reading rounding as signal) and ±3% (looser; risks absorbing the bottom edge of meaningful META decel). Both rejected as worse on the seed set.</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4>\n  <p>If backtest direction-agreement is &lt;60% AND ≥30% of misses occur on |YoY|&lt;3% samples, the deadband may be widened to ±3% as the single permitted in-sample retune.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"const-card\" id=\"c2\">\n  <h3><span class=\"const-id\">C2</span> Strong-magnitude threshold: 8% YoY</h3>\n  <div class=\"const-formula\">If <code>abs(yoy_change) &gt;= 0.08</code>, <code>magnitude = \"strong\"</code>; otherwise <code>magnitude = \"mild\"</code>.</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4>\n  <p>8% separates META's normal DAP cadence (mid-single-digit YoY) from clearly out-of-trend prints (≥8% accel or decel signals a regime change). RDDT's print cadence is structurally higher (mid-teens to mid-twenties), so RDDT prints land in <code>strong</code> by default — which matches the intuition: a high-growth app's signal is louder per unit of YoY-change. Spike samples (META 4% → mild; RDDT 17% → strong) match the intended classification.</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4>\n  <p>Per-ticker thresholds (calibrated to each ticker's baseline volatility). Rejected for v1 because (a) it doubles the surface area to tune without backtest evidence supporting the lift, and (b) the global threshold's failure mode is well-defined — it under-weights large-cap signals and over-weights high-growth signals. OQ2 in the design doc explicitly defers per-ticker calibration to post-backtest evidence.</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4>\n  <p>If backtest shows direction-agreement is materially asymmetric between mild and strong samples (e.g. strong agrees ≥70%, mild agrees ≤50%), this is the prompt to move to per-ticker thresholds — but that revision creates a <em>new</em> ADR with fresh data, not an in-sample retune of this one.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"const-card\" id=\"c3\">\n  <h3><span class=\"const-id\">C3</span> Input cap: ±4% on Year-1 revenue anchor</h3>\n  <div class=\"const-formula\"><code>base_anchor_multiplier = 1 + direction × (0.02 if mild else 0.04)</code> → ∈ [0.96, 1.04].</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4>\n  <p>±4% caps the modifier's input perturbation at a level smaller than the typical quarter-to-quarter analyst-consensus revision (which empirically runs ±2–6% on the seed set). The modifier should nudge the Y1 anchor in the direction of where consensus is <em>about to revise</em>, not anticipate the revision's full magnitude. ±4% is the largest input perturbation that, combined with the C4 output cap, holds the design's claim that the modifier is a directional nudge rather than a re-forecast.</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4>\n  <p>±2% (too small — strong signals get the same nudge as mild ones; loses the magnitude band) and ±6% (too large — under leveraged DCF sensitivities the output cap C4 binds frequently, defeating the input cap's purpose). ±4% is the value where the input cap and the output cap are both occasionally binding, which is the desired regime.</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4>\n  <p>If, post-Task 6, the input cap binds on &gt;40% of samples while the output cap binds on &lt;10%, the input cap is too tight; if the output cap binds on &gt;30% while the input cap is rarely the active constraint, the input cap is too loose. Either case prompts re-authoring (new ADR).</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"const-card\" id=\"c4\">\n  <h3><span class=\"const-id\">C4</span> Output cap: ≤5% on base IV</h3>\n  <div class=\"const-formula\">Post-application, if <code>abs(base_iv_after / base_iv_before − 1) &gt; 0.05</code>, clamp the multiplier downward until the IV impact is ≤5%, and record <code>status: \"clamped\"</code> with <code>clamped_from: &lt;original_multiplier&gt;</code> in the JSON.</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4>\n  <p>The modifier exists to nudge IV in the direction of expected consensus revision, not to dominate the valuation. A 5% IV ceiling means the modifier can never single-handedly flip a base-case verdict; it can only shade it. Combined with C3, this enforces two-level defense in depth: an input bound that's easy to reason about and an output bound that catches DCF leverage edge cases.</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4>\n  <p>A single output cap with no input cap (simpler, but loses the auditable narrative — \"we said the modifier was a ±4% nudge\" reads better than \"we said it was a ≤5% effect, derived\"). Rejected for explainability reasons.</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4>\n  <p>If <code>engagement_modifier_base_iv_impact_pct</code> regularly exceeds 5% pre-clamp (&gt;20% of applied runs) the cap is doing real work — that's fine, no revision. If the cap <em>never</em> binds, C3 is conservative enough that C4 is dead weight, and a future ADR may simplify.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"const-card\" id=\"c5\">\n  <h3><span class=\"const-id\">C5</span> Base-scenario only</h3>\n  <div class=\"const-formula\">The multiplier is applied to the <strong>base</strong> scenario's Y1 anchor only. Bull and bear scenarios are unaffected.</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4>\n  <p>Bull and bear scenarios have their own intentional bias by construction (optimistic / pessimistic growth assumptions). Compounding an additional engagement-direction nudge onto them double-counts: a positive engagement print already partially explains why the bull scenario is bull-shaped. The modifier targets the <em>central</em> estimate where the analyst-consensus-revision signal it tracks is most directly priced. This also preserves scenario-axis independence — the bull–base–bear spread is unchanged by the modifier (it shifts the central value, not the width).</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4>\n  <p>Apply to all three scenarios (more dramatic, more confusing). Apply only to bear when KPI is negative / only to bull when KPI is positive (asymmetric — risk: looks like cherry-picking to reinforce conclusions). Both rejected. The base-only choice resolves OQ5 in the spike-outputs note.</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4>\n  <p>If post-launch the user reports that bull/bear scenarios feel \"stale\" relative to base after a strong engagement print, the resolution is more likely a UI clarification (the spread shifts with base, since bull = base × multiplier and bear = base × multiplier) than an ADR change. Revisit only on direct evidence that scenario-axis independence is causing decision-confusion.</p>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"const-card\" id=\"c6\">\n  <h3><span class=\"const-id\">C6</span> Backtest-pass threshold: 60% direction agreement on ≥24 valid samples</h3>\n  <div class=\"const-formula\">NFR7 ships only if Task 6's revision-direction agreement is <strong>≥60%</strong> on <strong>≥24 valid ticker-quarter samples</strong>.</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4>\n  <p>The null hypothesis for direction agreement is 50% (coin-flip). For the modifier to be load-bearing it must demonstrate a meaningful lift above coin-flip. ≥10pp above 50% is the smallest lift where, on a 24-sample test, the result is unlikely to be noise (binomial p-value ≈ 0.27 for exactly 60% on n=24; tightens fast as n grows toward the 40-sample target). 60% is therefore: high enough to rule out coin-flip-with-noise, low enough that a single calibration miss doesn't sink an otherwise-real signal, and clear enough to be a falsifiable up-front commitment.</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4>\n  <p>55% (closer to noise floor; not enough lift to claim the modifier is doing work). 65–70% (too aggressive for v1; the seed-set sample is small and even a real signal might not clear it). Both rejected.</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4>\n  <p>The threshold itself is not revisable in-sample. If Task 6 returns 55–59%, the design-doc Week-2 → Week-3 gate fires: one in-sample constant revision (most likely C1 or C2) is permitted, then the backtest re-runs against the same threshold. A second sub-60% result ships the modifier disabled-by-default with <code>--engagement-modifier</code> as opt-in.</p>\n</div>\n\n<h2 id=\"consequences\">Consequences</h2>\n\n<div class=\"conseq-block conseq-positive\">\n  <h4>Positive</h4>\n  <ul>\n    <li>Task 6 backtest runs against fixed constants — its hit rate is an honest test of the underlying signal, not of constant tuning.</li>\n    <li>The audit trail (<code>stages.model.engagement_modifier</code> JSON) records every constant the run used, so downstream replay produces identical output.</li>\n    <li>The two-cap structure (C3 input + C4 output) is explainable to a reader in two sentences without requiring DCF intuition.</li>\n    <li>Pre-registering 60% as the kill criterion makes the spike→build gate falsifiable on evidence rather than negotiation.</li>\n  </ul>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"conseq-block conseq-negative\">\n  <h4>Negative</h4>\n  <ul>\n    <li>Global thresholds (C1, C2) under-fit the variance between META-class and RDDT-class signals. The design accepts this for v1 in exchange for not double-spending the in-sample revision budget on per-ticker calibration before the backtest has run.</li>\n    <li>Base-only application (C5) means a strong engagement print does not visibly perturb the bull or bear scenarios — users who don't read the doc may miss why. Mitigation: the report UI surfaces the modifier in the base column with a tooltip noting the scenario-axis-independence rationale (Task 11/12 scope).</li>\n    <li>One-revision budget creates pressure to \"spend\" it on the first sub-threshold result rather than investigate root cause. Mitigation: a revision requires explaining which constant moved and why, recorded as a superseding ADR.</li>\n  </ul>\n</div>\n\n<div class=\"conseq-block conseq-neutral\">\n  <h4>Neutral / open</h4>\n  <ul>\n    <li>OQ1 (revenue vs. FCF-margin lever) is not resolved by this ADR. The Y1 anchor named throughout is the <strong>revenue</strong> anchor per the design-doc default. If Task 9 (live wiring) surfaces a reason to perturb the FCF-margin path instead, that is a new ADR.</li>\n  </ul>\n</div>\n\n<h2 id=\"linkage\">Linkage</h2>\n<ul>\n  <li>Design doc: <code>docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/design-doc.md</code> §Constraints (FR3, FR4), §Recommended approach §3.</li>\n  <li>Spike outputs: <code>docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/spike-outputs.md</code> (META and RDDT classification samples).</li>\n  <li>Feasibility note: <code>docs/design-docs/engagement-kpi-enrichment/backtest-data-source.md</code> (Task 4 — data-source path used by C6's backtest).</li>\n  <li>Task plan: <code>docs/tasks/engagement-kpi-enrichment.md</code> (Task 5 = this ADR; Task 6 = backtest gated on it).</li>\n</ul>\n\n</main>\n<footer>Generated by render-html. Re-render the source markdown to update.</footer>\n</div>\n<script>\n  const links = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('nav.toc a'));\n  const targets = links.map(a => document.getElementById(a.getAttribute('href').slice(1))).filter(Boolean);\n  const byId = new Map(targets.map((el, i) => [el.id, links[i]]));\n  const io = new IntersectionObserver(entries => {\n    for (const e of entries) {\n      if (e.isIntersecting) {\n        links.forEach(a => a.classList.remove('active'));\n        byId.get(e.target.id)?.classList.add('active');\n      }\n    }\n  }, { rootMargin: '-30% 0px -60% 0px' });\n  targets.forEach(t => io.observe(t));\n</script>\n</body>\n</html>\n"
}
```

> AGENT

Three docs rendered. Quick findings on what worked vs what the catalogue needs to learn:

**Rendered:**
1. `~/src/nestl/docs/roadmap.html` — Theme distribution table got P8 ICE chips + slot badges. Now/Next/Later H2s kept as prose (see finding below). Problem blocks got P3 callouts.
2. `~/src/nestl/docs/adr/001-rails-monolith.html` — Context/Decision/Consequences in P3 callouts. Rationale's "Why X" bold-paragraph subsections got a styled treatment that isn't currently in the catalogue.
3. `~/src/stock-review/docs/adrs/engagement-modifier-constants.html` — Same P3 callouts; YAML frontmatter rendered as a metadata grid; C1–C6 sub-blocks inside Decision became styled "constant cards" (not in catalogue); Consequences's Positive/Negative/Neutral buckets got coloured bands.

**Catalogue gaps I had to invent on the fly — candidates for new patterns or detect-rule tweaks:**

- **P7 misfires on rich Now/Next/Later.** The roadmap has Now / Next / Later as H2s but their bodies are prose-heavy (Problem callouts, Appetite, ICE) rather than bullets. P7's current detect rule would falsely match and collapse all the prose into bullets. Catalogue needs a guard: *only apply kanban when each column's body is predominantly a `<ul>`/`<ol>`*.
- **P8 detect too strict.** Current rule needs Impact + Confidence + Ease as separate columns. Real tables (the roadmap) use a single `ICE` column. Loosen to: `ICE` column OR (Impact AND Confidence AND Ease).
- **Frontmatter metadata grid** isn't in the catalogue. Both ADRs benefit from a styled YAML/frontmatter block at the top.
- **Status pill** ("Accepted" / "Proposed" / etc.) is its own micro-pattern worth promoting from one-off inline CSS.
- **Rationale "Why X" sub-sections** (bold paragraph + bullet list, no H3) — common ADR shape. Could be a P9 "rationale columns" or just an opinionated treatment of "bold-paragraph-headed lists".
- **Constant cards (C1, C2…)** in the stock-review ADR — same shape as "Reasoning / Alternative / Revision trigger" repeated per item. This is a pattern: *labelled-item card with repeated sub-fields*. Maybe a new P9 "labelled rubric cards".
- **Consequences sub-buckets** (Positive / Negative / Neutral / open) — extension of P3 callouts. Could be encoded as P3 variants triggered by H3 inside Consequences.

Want me to (a) keep rendering more docs to gather more evidence, (b) fold these findings into `references/html-patterns.md` now and commit, or (c) hand off so you can browser-eyeball the three renders first and tell me which patterns feel right/wrong?

269	
270	## P8 — ICE scores → coloured score chips
271	
272	**Detect.** Markdown table whose header row contains EITHER (a) `Impact`, `Confidence`, and `Ease` (in any order, plus an optional `Score` column), OR (b) a single `ICE` column alongside at least one of `Confidence` / `Slot` / `Theme` / `Kano` — real roadmaps frequently collapse the three sub-scores into one `ICE` total rather than listing each. Numeric cells are graded on the same red/amber/green band regardless of column. Em-dash placeholders (`—`) render as a muted chip.
273	
274	**HTML.** Numeric cells become chips coloured by value (red 1–3, amber 4–6, green 7–10).
275	
276	```html
277	<table class="p-ice">
278	  <thead><tr><th>Idea</th><th>Impact</th><th>Confidence</th><th>Ease</th><th>ICE</th></tr></thead>
279	  <tbody>
280	    <tr>
281	      <td>Onboarding revamp</td>
282	      <td><span class="p-chip" data-band="green">9</span></td>
283	      <td><span class="p-chip" data-band="amber">5</span></td>
284	      <td><span class="p-chip" data-band="red">3</span></td>
285	      <td><span class="p-chip" data-band="amber">135</span></td>
286	    </tr>
287	  </tbody>
288	</table>
289	```
290	
291	**CSS.**
292	
293	```css
294	.p-ice .p-chip { display: inline-block; min-width: 28px; padding: 2px 8px; border-radius: 999px; font-family: ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, monospace; font-size: 12px; text-align: center; font-weight: 600; }
295	.p-chip[data-band="red"]   { background: rgba(220,53,69,0.18); color: #dc3545; }
296	.p-chip[data-band="amber"] { background: rgba(253,126,20,0.18); color: #fd7e14; }
297	.p-chip[data-band="green"] { background: rgba(25,135,84,0.18); color: #198754; }
298	.p-ice td:not(:first-child) { text-align: center; }
299	```
300	
301	**Accessibility.** Colour is supplemental; the numeric value is the source of truth. Chips render as plain numbers in print and to screen readers.
302	
303	---
304	
305	## Fallback
306	
307	A section that matches no pattern renders with the shell's standard prose styles (`<h2>`, `<h3>`, `<p>`, `<ul>`, `<table>`, `<pre>`). Fallback is the default, not the exception — false-positive pattern matches degrade review trust faster than missing treatments.
308	
309	## Adding a new pattern
310	
311	When a new pattern earns its slot:
312	
313	1. Add a section here following the same six-part structure: heading, **Detect**, **HTML**, **CSS**, **Accessibility**, brief rationale.
314	2. Number it sequentially (`P9 — …`) so cross-references stay stable.
315	3. Confirm the CSS only adds new selectors prefixed `p-` — it must not override any rule in `html-skeleton.md`.
316	4. If the detection signal could overlap an existing pattern, document the precedence rule explicitly. The first-match-wins ordering of this file is the resolution mechanism.
317	
```

If the detection signal could overlap an existing pattern, document the precedence rule explicitly. The first-match-wins ordering of this file is the resolution mechanism.",
  "new_string": "## P9 — YAML frontmatter → metadata grid\n\n**Detect.** Source markdown begins with a YAML frontmatter block delimited by `---` on its own line, before the H1. Common in ADRs and design docs (fields like `id`, `status`, `date`, `authors`, `linear`, `supersedes`).\n\n**HTML.** Render as a definition list immediately under the page title and the provenance meta line — before any body content.\n\n```html\n<dl class=\"p-frontmatter\">\n  <dt>id</dt><dd>ADR-engagement-modifier-constants</dd>\n  <dt>status</dt><dd><span class=\"p-status p-status-accepted\">accepted</span></dd>\n  <dt>date</dt><dd>2026-05-14</dd>\n  <dt>authors</dt><dd>Anton Babushkin</dd>\n</dl>\n```\n\n**CSS.**\n\n```css\n.p-frontmatter { display: grid; grid-template-columns: auto 1fr; gap: 4px 14px; font-size: 13px; padding: 12px 14px; background: rgba(127,127,127,0.04); border-radius: 6px; margin-bottom: 24px; }\n.p-frontmatter dt { color: var(--muted); text-transform: uppercase; font-size: 11px; letter-spacing: 0.04em; margin: 0; }\n.p-frontmatter dd { margin: 0; font-family: ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, monospace; font-size: 12.5px; }\n```\n\nThe `status` field gets the P10 status-pill treatment automatically; other field values render as plain text. **Do not** drop frontmatter on the floor — readers use it to anchor the doc.\n\n**Accessibility.** Definition list is the right semantic tag for key-value metadata; screen readers announce each pair.\n\n---\n\n## P10 — Status / state values → coloured pill\n\n**Detect.** A frontmatter `status:` field, OR a top-of-doc bold line matching `/\\*\\*Status:\\*\\*\\s+(\\w+)/i`, OR a single-cell `Status` column in a table. Recognised values (case-insensitive): `accepted`, `proposed`, `draft`, `rejected`, `superseded`, `deprecated`, `flagged`, `ok`.\n\n**HTML.**\n\n```html\n<span class=\"p-status p-status-accepted\">Accepted</span>\n```\n\n**CSS.**\n\n```css\n.p-status { display: inline-block; padding: 2px 9px; border-radius: 999px; font-weight: 600; font-size: 11px; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.04em; }\n.p-status-accepted, .p-status-ok        { background: rgba(25,135,84,0.18);  color: #198754; }\n.p-status-proposed, .p-status-draft     { background: rgba(10,88,202,0.18);  color: #0a58ca; }\n.p-status-rejected, .p-status-deprecated{ background: rgba(220,53,69,0.18);  color: #dc3545; }\n.p-status-superseded, .p-status-flagged { background: rgba(253,126,20,0.18); color: #fd7e14; }\n```\n\nA pill is reusable wherever a state lives in the source — Slot columns (Now/Next/Later/Validation) in P7-rejected roadmap tables are a natural reuse site.\n\n**Accessibility.** The label text carries the meaning; colour is supplemental.\n\n---\n\n## P11 — Rationale \"Why X\" blocks → labelled list groups\n\n**Detect.** A section heading matches `/rationale/i` AND its body contains two-or-more bold-paragraph headers (`**Why X:**` / `**Why not Y:**`) each followed by a bullet list. Common in ADRs that consider multiple alternatives without dedicated H3s per option.\n\n**HTML.** Each bold paragraph becomes the heading of a styled group; the following list is the group's body.\n\n```html\n<section class=\"p-rationale\">\n  <div class=\"p-rationale-group\">\n    <h4>Why Rails</h4>\n    <ul><li>…</li></ul>\n  </div>\n  <div class=\"p-rationale-group\">\n    <h4>Why not a separate frontend</h4>\n    <ul><li>…</li></ul>\n  </div>\n</section>\n```\n\n**CSS.**\n\n```css\n.p-rationale-group { margin: 14px 0; }\n.p-rationale-group h4 { margin: 0 0 6px; font-size: 14px; color: var(--accent); font-weight: 600; }\n.p-rationale-group ul { margin: 0; padding-left: 22px; }\n```\n\n**Precedence.** P11 ranks below P1 (Alternatives). When both could match (`Rationale` section whose options also have `Pros:`/`Cons:`), P1's side-by-side comparison is the stronger treatment.\n\n**Accessibility.** `<h4>` keeps the headings in the document outline; lists keep list semantics.\n\n---\n\n## P12 — Labelled rubric cards (C1, C2, FR3 …) → repeated sub-field cards\n\n**Detect.** Two or more sibling H3s whose text matches `/^([A-Z]+\\d+)\\s+[—–-]\\s+/` (e.g. `C1 — Deadband: ±2%`, `FR3 — Idempotent writes`, `OQ5 — …`), AND each section repeats the same set of bold-paragraph sub-fields (e.g. **Reasoning**, **Alternative considered**, **Revision trigger**). The identifier prefix (`C`, `FR`, `OQ`, `NFR`, `ADR`) is rendered as a pill on the card header.\n\n**HTML.**\n\n```html\n<div class=\"p-rubric\" id=\"c1\">\n  <h3><span class=\"p-rubric-id\">C1</span> Deadband: ±2% YoY</h3>\n  <div class=\"p-rubric-formula\">If <code>abs(yoy_change) &lt; 0.02</code>, set <code>direction = 0</code> …</div>\n  <h4>Reasoning</h4><p>…</p>\n  <h4>Alternative considered</h4><p>…</p>\n  <h4>Revision trigger</h4><p>…</p>\n</div>\n```\n\nThe first paragraph (often a one-line formal definition or formula) becomes `p-rubric-formula`. Subsequent bold-paragraph sub-fields become `<h4>` headings inside the card.\n\n**CSS.**\n\n```css\n.p-rubric { border: 1px solid var(--rule); border-left: 4px solid var(--accent); border-radius: 6px; padding: 14px 18px; margin: 16px 0; background: rgba(127,127,127,0.02); }\n.p-rubric h3 { margin-top: 0; display: flex; gap: 12px; align-items: baseline; color: var(--fg); }\n.p-rubric h3 .p-rubric-id { font-family: ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, monospace; font-size: 13px; padding: 2px 8px; background: var(--accent); color: white; border-radius: 4px; }\n.p-rubric h4 { font-size: 13px; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.04em; color: var(--muted); margin: 12px 0 4px; font-weight: 600; }\n.p-rubric-formula { background: rgba(10,88,202,0.06); border-left: 3px solid var(--accent); padding: 8px 12px; margin: 8px 0; font-family: ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, monospace; font-size: 13px; }\n```\n\n**Why a dedicated pattern.** Plain-prose rendering of a 6-card ADR section (C1–C6 with Reasoning / Alternative / Revision trigger each) produces a wall of repeating H3+H4 that the reader has to mentally segment. The card boundary plus the identifier pill makes \"which constant am I reading\" answerable at a glance — which is most of what reviewers want when scanning a pre-registered constants ADR.\n\n**Accessibility.** Heading levels (`<h3>` outer, `<h4>` inner) preserve the document outline; the card's visual boundary is decorative.\n\n---\n\n## Fallback\n\nA section that matches no pattern renders with the shell's standard prose styles (`<h2>`, `<h3>`, `<p>`, `<ul>`, `<table>`, `<pre>`). Fallback is the default, not the exception — false-positive pattern matches degrade review trust faster than missing treatments.\n\n## Adding a new pattern\n\nWhen a new pattern earns its slot:\n\n1. Add a section here following the same six-part structure: heading, **Detect**, **HTML**, **CSS**, **Accessibility**, brief rationale.\n2. Number it sequentially (`P13 — …`) so cross-references stay stable.\n3. Confirm the CSS only adds new selectors prefixed `p-` — it must not override any rule in `html-skeleton.md`.\n4. If the detection signal could overlap an existing pattern, document the precedence rule explicitly. The first-match-wins ordering of this file is the resolution mechanism."
}

```
1	# ADR-001: Ruby on Rails Monolith
2	
3	**Status:** Accepted
4	**Date:** 31 March 2026
5	
6	## Context
7	
8	We need to choose a tech stack for Nestl, a single-user rental listing evaluator. The app has a standard web UI (listing feed, detail pages, forms), background job processing (scraping, email ingestion), and no complex client-side interactivity. It will be built and maintained by a solo developer.
9	
10	## Decision
11	
12	Build as a Ruby on Rails 8 monolith using Rails defaults wherever possible.
13	
14	## Rationale
15	
16	**Why Rails:**
17	- Full-stack framework: models, views, controllers, jobs, mailers, email ingestion (Action Mailbox) all in one codebase
18	- Rails 8 defaults (SQLite, Solid Queue, Solid Cache, Solid Cable, Kamal) eliminate external dependencies — no Redis, no Postgres, no separate job runner
19	- Mature scraping ecosystem (Nokogiri is bundled, HTTP.rb for HTTP client)
20	- Convention-over-configuration means less decision fatigue for a solo developer
21	- The app is fundamentally CRUD + background jobs + server-rendered pages — Rails' sweet spot
22	
23	**Why not a separate frontend (React/Next.js):**
24	- The UI is listing cards, detail pages, and forms — no complex client-side state
25	- A separate frontend doubles the deployment surface, adds a build pipeline, and introduces API serialisation overhead
26	- Hotwire (Turbo + Stimulus) handles the few dynamic interactions needed (shortlist actions, filters) without a JS framework
27	
28	**Why not Python/FastAPI:**
29	- FastAPI is backend-only; would still need a frontend framework or templating layer
30	- Django would be comparable to Rails but the developer prefers Ruby
31	
32	**Why SQLite over Postgres:**
33	- Single-user app with low write volume (tens of listings per day)
34	- Rails 8 has first-class SQLite support including for queues, cache, and WebSockets
35	- Zero-ops: no database server to manage
36	- Can migrate to Postgres later if multi-user support is added
37	
38	## Consequences
39	
40	- All code lives in one repo and deploys as one unit
41	- Background jobs (scraping, email processing, evaluation) run in the same process via Solid Queue
42	- Deployment is a single Docker container via Kamal to a VPS
43	- If we later need a separate API (e.g. for a native mobile app), we can add API controllers to the same Rails app
44	
```

}\n  section.chapter .num {\n    position: absolute; left: -88px; top: -2px;\n    font: 700 64px/1 \"Iowan Old Style\", Georgia, serif;\n    color: var(--rule);\n    user-select: none;\n  }\n  @media (max-width: 880px) {\n    section.chapter .num { position: static; display: block; font-size: 38px; margin-bottom: 4px; color: var(--muted); }\n  }\n  h2 {\n    font: 700 26px/1.3 \"Iowan Old Style\", Georgia, serif;\n    margin: 0 0 20px;\n    letter-spacing: -0.005em;\n  }\n  p { margin: 0 0 18px; }\n  p:first-of-type::first-letter { /* subtle */ }\n\n  /* Lift-quote treatment for one-line decisions */\n  .lift {\n    font: italic 24px/1.45 \"Iowan Old Style\", Georgia, serif;\n    color: var(--decision);\n    border-left: 3px solid var(--decision);\n    padding: 8px 0 8px 24px;\n    margin: 0 0 12px;\n  }\n\n  /* Rationale: editorial sub-heads, hanging dashes */\n  .reason { margin: 30px 0; }\n  .reason h3 {\n    font: 600 13px/1.3 ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;\n    letter-spacing: 0.12em; text-transform: uppercase;\n    color: var(--accent); margin: 0 0 10px;\n  }\n  .reason ul { list-style: none; padding: 0; margin: 0; }\n  .reason li { position: relative; padding-left: 22px; margin: 8px 0; }\n  .reason li::before { content: \"—\"; position: absolute; left: 0; color: var(--muted); }\n\n  /* Consequences as numbered editorial list */\n  .conseq ol { list-style: none; counter-reset: c; padding: 0; margin: 0; }\n  .conseq li { counter-increment: c; position: relative; padding-left: 42px; margin: 14px 0; }\n  .conseq li::before {\n    content: counter(c, decimal-leading-zero);\n    position: absolute; left: 0; top: 2px;\n    font: 600 12px/1 ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif;\n    color: var(--conseq); letter-spacing: 0.05em;\n  }\n\n  footer { margin-top: 100px; padding-top: 24px; border-top: 1px solid var(--rule); font: 12px/1.5 ui-sans-serif, system-ui, sans-serif; color: var(--muted); text-align: center; }\n  footer em { font-style: italic; }\n</style>\n</head>\n<body>\n<article class=\"page\">\n  <header class=\"masthead\">\n    <div class=\"eyebrow\">Architecture Decision Record · No. 001</div>\n    <h1>Ruby on Rails Monolith</h1>\n    <p class=\"byline\"><span class=\"status\">Accepted</span>Recorded the thirty-first of March, twenty twenty-six.</p>\n  </header>\n\n  <section class=\"chapter\">\n    <span class=\"num\">I</span>\n    <h2>Context</h2>\n    <p>We need to choose a tech stack for Nestl, a single-user rental listing evaluator. The app has a standard web UI (listing feed, detail pages, forms), background job processing (scraping, email ingestion), and no complex client-side interactivity. It will be built and maintained by a solo developer.</p>\n  </section>\n\n  <section class=\"chapter\">\n    <span class=\"num\">II</span>\n    <h2>Decision</h2>\n    <p class=\"lift\">Build as a Ruby on Rails 8 monolith using Rails defaults wherever possible.</p>\n  </section>\n\n  <section class=\"chapter\">\n    <span class=\"num\">III</span>\n    <h2>Rationale</h2>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why Rails</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>Full-stack framework: models, views, controllers, jobs, mailers, email ingestion (Action Mailbox) all in one codebase</li>\n        <li>Rails 8 defaults (SQLite, Solid Queue, Solid Cache, Solid Cable, Kamal) eliminate external dependencies — no Redis, no Postgres, no separate job runner</li>\n        <li>Mature scraping ecosystem (Nokogiri is bundled, HTTP.rb for HTTP client)</li>\n        <li>Convention-over-configuration means less decision fatigue for a solo developer</li>\n        <li>The app is fundamentally CRUD + background jobs + server-rendered pages — Rails' sweet spot</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why not a separate frontend</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>The UI is listing cards, detail pages, and forms — no complex client-side state</li>\n        <li>A separate frontend doubles the deployment surface, adds a build pipeline, and introduces API serialisation overhead</li>\n        <li>Hotwire (Turbo + Stimulus) handles the few dynamic interactions needed (shortlist actions, filters) without a JS framework</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why not Python / FastAPI</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>FastAPI is backend-only; would still need a frontend framework or templating layer</li>\n        <li>Django would be comparable to Rails but the developer prefers Ruby</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why SQLite over Postgres</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>Single-user app with low write volume (tens of listings per day)</li>\n        <li>Rails 8 has first-class SQLite support including for queues, cache, and WebSockets</li>\n        <li>Zero-ops: no database server to manage</li>\n        <li>Can migrate to Postgres later if multi-user support is added</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n  </section>\n\n  <section class=\"chapter conseq\">\n    <span class=\"num\">IV</span>\n    <h2>Consequences</h2>\n    <ol>\n      <li>All code lives in one repo and deploys as one unit.</li>\n      <li>Background jobs (scraping, email processing, evaluation) run in the same process via Solid Queue.</li>\n      <li>Deployment is a single Docker container via Kamal to a VPS.</li>\n      <li>If we later need a separate API (e.g. for a native mobile app), we can add API controllers to the same Rails app.</li>\n    </ol>\n  </section>\n\n  <footer>\n    Rendered from <em>docs/adr/001-rails-monolith.md</em> · Sample A — Editorial\n  </footer>\n</article>\n</body>\n</html>\n"
}

for (const e of entries) {\n      if (e.isIntersecting) {\n        links.forEach(a => a.classList.remove('active'));\n        byId.get(e.target.id)?.classList.add('active');\n      }\n    }\n  }, { }\n  .reason h3 {\n    font: 500 13px/1.4 \"Source Serif Pro\", Georgia, serif;\n    font-variant: small-caps;\n    letter-spacing: 0.06em;\n    margin: 22px 0 8px;\n    color: var(--ink);\n  }\n  .reason ul { margin: 0; padding: 0; list-style: none; }\n  .reason li {\n    position: relative; padding-left: 22px; margin: 6px 0;\n    font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.65; color: var(--ink-soft);\n  }\n  .reason li::before { content: \"·\"; position: absolute; left: 8px; top: -4px; font-size: 22px; color: var(--muted); }\n\n  /* Consequences: numbered, en-dash separated */\n  .conseq ol { list-style: none; padding: 0; margin: 0; counter-reset: ck; }\n  .conseq li {\n    counter-increment: ck;\n    padding: 12px 0 12px 44px; position: relative;\n    border-top: 1px solid var(--rule);\n    font-size: 16px; line-height: 1.6; color: var(--ink-soft);\n  }\n  .conseq li:last-child { border-bottom: 1px solid var(--rule); }\n  .conseq li::before {\n    content: counter(ck) \".\";\n    position: absolute; left: 8px; top: 12px;\n    font: italic 14px/1.6 Georgia, serif;\n    color: var(--muted);\n  }\n\n  /* Colophon */\n  .colophon {\n    margin-top: 96px; padding-top: 24px;\n    border-top: 1px solid var(--rule);\n    text-align: center;\n    font: italic 12px/1.6 Georgia, serif; color: var(--muted);\n  }\n  .colophon code { font-style: normal; font-family: ui-monospace, monospace; font-size: 11px; }\n\n  @media print {\n    body { background: white; color: black; }\n    .page { padding: 48px 40px; max-width: none; }\n  }\n</style>\n</head>\n<body>\n<article class=\"page\">\n\n  <header class=\"title-block\">\n    <div class=\"doc-id\">Architecture Decision Record &nbsp;·&nbsp; No. 001</div>\n    <h1>Ruby on Rails Monolith</h1>\n    <p class=\"meta-line\">\n      <span>Status: <em>Accepted</em></span>\n      <span>31 March 2026</span>\n      <span>Nestl</span>\n    </p>\n  </header>\n\n  <section class=\"body\" id=\"context\">\n    <h2>Context <span class=\"sec-num\">§ I</span></h2>\n    <p>We need to choose a tech stack for Nestl, a single-user rental listing evaluator. The app has a standard web UI (listing feed, detail pages, forms), background job processing (scraping, email ingestion), and no complex client-side interactivity. It will be built and maintained by a solo developer.</p>\n  </section>\n\n  <section class=\"body\" id=\"decision\">\n    <h2>Decision <span class=\"sec-num\">§ II</span></h2>\n    <p class=\"decision-callout\">Build as a Ruby on Rails 8 monolith using Rails defaults wherever possible.</p>\n  </section>\n\n  <section class=\"body\" id=\"rationale\">\n    <h2>Rationale <span class=\"sec-num\">§ III</span></h2>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why Rails</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>Full-stack framework: models, views, controllers, jobs, mailers, email ingestion (Action Mailbox) all in one codebase.</li>\n        <li>Rails 8 defaults (SQLite, Solid Queue, Solid Cache, Solid Cable, Kamal) eliminate external dependencies — no Redis, no Postgres, no separate job runner.</li>\n        <li>Mature scraping ecosystem (Nokogiri is bundled, HTTP.rb for HTTP client).</li>\n        <li>Convention-over-configuration means less decision fatigue for a solo developer.</li>\n        <li>The app is fundamentally CRUD + background jobs + server-rendered pages — Rails' sweet spot.</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why not a separate frontend</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>The UI is listing cards, detail pages, and forms — no complex client-side state.</li>\n        <li>A separate frontend doubles the deployment surface, adds a build pipeline, and introduces API serialisation overhead.</li>\n        <li>Hotwire (Turbo + Stimulus) handles the few dynamic interactions needed (shortlist actions, filters) without a JS framework.</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why not Python / FastAPI</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>FastAPI is backend-only; would still need a frontend framework or templating layer.</li>\n        <li>Django would be comparable to Rails but the developer prefers Ruby.</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n\n    <div class=\"reason\">\n      <h3>Why SQLite over Postgres</h3>\n      <ul>\n        <li>Single-user app with low write volume (tens of listings per day).</li>\n        <li>Rails 8 has first-class SQLite support including for queues, cache, and WebSockets.</li>\n        <li>Zero-ops: no database server to manage.</li>\n        <li>Can migrate to Postgres later if multi-user support is added.</li>\n      </ul>\n    </div>\n  </section>\n\n  <section class=\"body conseq\" id=\"consequences\">\n    <h2>Consequences <span class=\"sec-num\">§ IV</span></h2>\n    <ol>\n      <li>All code lives in one repo and deploys as one unit.</li>\n      <li>Background jobs (scraping, email processing, evaluation) run in the same process via Solid Queue.</li>\n      <li>Deployment is a single Docker container via Kamal to a VPS.</li>\n      <li>If we later need a separate API (e.g. for a native mobile app), we can add API controllers to the same Rails app.</li>\n    </ol>\n  </section>\n\n  <footer class=\"colophon\">\n    Rendered from <code>docs/adr/001-rails-monolith.md</code> · Sample C — Whitepaper\n  </footer>\n\n</article>\n</body>\n</html>\n"
}

1	---
2	name: html-skeleton

4	  Self-contained HTML shell used by the render-html skill. Defines the single-file
5	  document template: inline CSS (system font, sticky TOC sidebar, prose column,

11	---
12	
13	# HTML skeleton
14	
15	The render-html skill wraps every markdown body in the shell below. The shell is the **only** mechanism by which output styling and navigation arrive — no external CSS, no external JS, no remote images. That rule is load-bearing for the skill's "opens via `file://` with no network" guarantee; reviewers attach these files to emails and store them in private wikis where CDN URLs silently break.
16	
17	## The hard rule
18	
19	Anything injected into the rendered output must satisfy all three:
20	
21	- **No `<link rel="stylesheet">`.** All CSS lives in the inline `<style>` block.
22	- **No `<script src=...>`.** All JS lives in the inline `<script>` block at the end of `<body>`.
23	- **No remote `<img>` URLs.** Inline SVG is preferred; base64 data URIs are allowed when an SVG is impractical. Remote URLs (`http:`, `https:`, protocol-relative `//`) are not.
24	
25	If a future pattern needs richer interactivity (charts, diagrams, syntax highlighting), inline the minimal library or hand-write the SVG. Adding a CDN is not an option.
26	
27	## Substitution tokens
28	
29	The skill substitutes these into the shell at render time. Treat them as the contract between the skill's workflow and this reference.
30	
31	| Token | Source | Notes |
32	|---|---|---|
33	| `{{title}}` | The source markdown's first H1 | Goes in both `<title>` and the page `<h1>`. |
34	| `{{source-path}}` | Path of the input `.md` file, relative to the repo root if possible | Shown in the provenance meta line. |
35	| `{{render-date}}` | ISO date (YYYY-MM-DD) of the render | Provenance only — do not include time, so re-renders on the same day produce no diff. |
36	| `{{toc-entries}}` | One `<li>` per H2/H3 from the source, with class `h2` or `h3` and an `href` to the heading's slug | Slugs are kebab-case from the heading text, deduplicated with `-2`, `-3` if needed. H1 is the title, not a TOC entry. |
37	| `{{body-html}}` | The markdown body converted to HTML, with H2/H3 carrying `id="<slug>"` matching the TOC links | Pattern-specific treatments (later iterations) substitute richer markup here. |
38	
39	## The shell
40	
41	```html
42	<!doctype html>
43	<html lang="en">
44	<head>
45	<meta charset="utf-8">
46	<meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1">
47	<title>{{title}}</title>
48	<style>
49	  :root { --fg: #111; --muted: #666; --bg: #fff; --rule: #e5e5e5; --accent: #0a58ca; }
50	  @media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) {
51	    :root { --fg: #eee; --muted: #999; --bg: #111; --rule: #2a2a2a; --accent: #6ea8fe; }
52	  }
53	  html, body { margin: 0; padding: 0; background: var(--bg); color: var(--fg); }
54	  body { font: 16px/1.55 ui-sans-serif, system-ui, -apple-system, "Segoe UI", Roboto, sans-serif; }
55	  .layout { display: grid; grid-template-columns: 260px 1fr; max-width: 1100px; margin: 0 auto; }
56	  nav.toc { position: sticky; top: 0; align-self: start; max-height: 100vh; overflow-y: auto; padding: 24px 16px; border-right: 1px solid var(--rule); font-size: 14px; }
57	  nav.toc h2 { font-size: 12px; text-transform: uppercase; letter-spacing: 0.05em; color: var(--muted); margin: 0 0 8px; }
58	  nav.toc ul { list-style: none; padding: 0; margin: 0; }
59	  nav.toc li { margin: 4px 0; }
60	  nav.toc li.h3 { padding-left: 12px; font-size: 13px; }
61	  nav.toc a { color: var(--fg); text-decoration: none; }
62	  nav.toc a:hover, nav.toc a.active { color: var(--accent); }
63	  main { padding: 32px 40px; max-width: 72ch; }
64	  main h1 { font-size: 28px; margin: 0 0 4px; }
65	  main .meta { color: var(--muted); font-size: 13px; margin-bottom: 32px; }
66	  main h2 { font-size: 22px; margin-top: 40px; padding-top: 8px; border-top: 1px solid var(--rule); }
67	  main h3 { font-size: 17px; margin-top: 24px; }
68	  main pre { background: rgba(127,127,127,0.08); padding: 12px 14px; border-radius: 6px; overflow-x: auto; font-size: 13px; }
69	  main code { font-family: ui-monospace, SFMono-Regular, Menlo, monospace; font-size: 0.92em; }
70	  main table { border-collapse: collapse; margin: 12px 0; }
71	  main th, main td { border: 1px solid var(--rule); padding: 6px 10px; text-align: left; }
72	  footer { grid-column: 1 / -1; padding: 24px 40px; color: var(--muted); font-size: 12px; border-top: 1px solid var(--rule); }
73	  @media print {
74	    nav.toc { display: none; }
75	    .layout { grid-template-columns: 1fr; }
76	  }
77	  @media (max-width: 720px) {
78	    .layout { grid-template-columns: 1fr; }
79	    nav.toc { position: static; max-height: none; border-right: none; border-bottom: 1px solid var(--rule); }
80	  }
81	</style>
82	</head>
83	<body>
84	<div class="layout">
85	<nav class="toc" aria-label="Table of contents">
86	<h2>Contents</h2>
87	<ul>
88	  {{toc-entries}}
89	</ul>
90	</nav>
91	<main>
92	<h1>{{title}}</h1>
93	<p class="meta">Rendered from <code>{{source-path}}</code> on {{render-date}}</p>
94	{{body-html}}
95	</main>
96	<footer>Generated by render-html. Re-render the source markdown to update.</footer>
97	</div>
98	<script>
99	  // Scrollspy: highlight the TOC entry for the section currently in view.
100	  const links = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('nav.toc a'));
101	  const targets = links.map(a => document.getElementById(a.getAttribute('href').slice(1))).filter(Boolean);
102	  const byId = new Map(targets.map((el, i) => [el.id, links[i]]));
103	  const io = new IntersectionObserver(entries => {
104	    for (const e of entries) {
105	      if (e.isIntersecting) {
106	        links.forEach(a => a.classList.remove('active'));
107	        byId.get(e.target.id)?.classList.add('active');
108	      }
109	    }
110	  }, { rootMargin: '-30% 0px -60% 0px' });
111	  targets.forEach(t => io.observe(t));
112	</script>
113	</body>
114	</html>
115	```
116	
117	## Design notes
118	
119	- **System font stack** — `ui-sans-serif, system-ui, -apple-system, "Segoe UI", Roboto, sans-serif`. Zero network, native to every platform, sensible fallbacks. Web fonts would be a CDN dependency.
120	- **Prose width cap** — `max-width: 72ch` on `<main>`. Long-line prose is fatigue-inducing; reviewers skim faster at typographic widths.
121	- **Sticky TOC** — `position: sticky; top: 0; max-height: 100vh; overflow-y: auto;`. Stays visible while scrolling, scrolls internally for very long TOCs.
122	- **Scrollspy** — IntersectionObserver-based, ~12 lines. Highlights the TOC entry for whichever section is in view. The `rootMargin: '-30% 0px -60% 0px'` biases the active region to the upper third of the viewport, so the active entry matches where the reader's eye actually is.
123	- **Dark mode** — `prefers-color-scheme: dark` media query swaps CSS custom properties. Honours OS setting; no toggle UI (out of scope for v0.1).
124	- **Print** — TOC hidden, grid collapses to single column. Reviewers who print to PDF for offline annotation get something usable.
125	- **Responsive** — below 720px the TOC moves above the body. The skill is review-oriented (desktop-first) but mobile shouldn't be broken.
126	
127	## When pattern treatments arrive
128	
129	Pattern-specific markup (side-by-side alternatives, kanban Now/Next/Later, callouts, dependency timelines) substitutes into `{{body-html}}` — the shell itself does not change. Pattern CSS extends the inline `<style>` block via additions, not replacements, so the prose path remains the default fallback for any markdown the skill does not classify.
130	
```
